**Judaism**[^cite_note-4] ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): יַהֲדוּת, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Hebrew): *Yahăḏūṯ*) is an [Abrahamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions), [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism), [ethnic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_religion) that comprises the collective [spiritual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual), cultural, and legal traditions of the [Jewish people](25955086.md).[^cite_note-JEjudaism-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2007511_quote:_"Judaism,_the_religion,_philosophy,_and_way_of_life_of_the_Jews."-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman20033-7] [Religious Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Jews) regard Judaism as their means of observing the [Mosaic covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic_covenant), which they believe was established between [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) and the Jewish people.[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-Knowledge_Resources:_Judaism-9] The religion is considered one of the earliest monotheistic religions.

Judaism as a religion and culture is founded upon a diverse body of texts, traditions, theologies, and worldviews. Among Judaism's core texts are the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) ([Biblical Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew_language): תּוֹרָה, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'Teaching'), the *[Nevi'im](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevi'im)* (נְבִיאִים, 'Prophets'), and the *[Ketuvim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketuvim)* (כְּתוּבִים, 'Writings'), which together compose the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible). In [Modern Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Hebrew), the Hebrew Bible is often referred to as the *Tanakh* (תַּנַ׳׳ךּ, *Tanaḵ*)—an acronym of its constituent divisions—or the *Miqra* (מִקְרָא, *Miqrāʾ*, '\[that which is] called out'). In addition to scripture, Jewish religious texts include the [Oral Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_Torah) (תּוֹרָה שֶׁבְּעַל־פֶּה, *Tōrā šebbəʿal-pe*, 'Torah by the mouth')[^cite_note-10], comprising the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah), [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud), [Tosefta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tosefta), and Jewish legal *[Midrashim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrash)* (מִדְרָשִׁים, 'Studies' or 'Expositions'); *[Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha)* (הֲלָכָה, 'the Way'), or Jewish law; *[Aggadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aggadah)* (אָגָּדָה, 'Narrative'); and [responsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_responsa_in_Judaism). The Hebrew word *Torah* specifically refers to the [Five Books of Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Books_of_Moses), but it can also be used as a general term for the entire body of rabbinic literature and any teachings based on those texts,[^cite_note-Aish.com-11] and encompasses a variety of interpretations.

There are a variety of [Jewish religious movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements), most of which emerged from [Rabbinic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck200378–92-12][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman2003-13][^cite_note-Brabbinic-14] which holds that God revealed his laws and commandments to [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses) on [Mount Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Sinai_\(Bible\)) in both the Written Torah (תּוֹרָה שֶׁבִּכְתָב, *Tōrā šebbīḵṯāv*) and Oral Torah.[^cite_note-What_is_the_oral_Torah?-15] Historically, all or part of this assertion was challenged by groups like the [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees#General) and practitioners of [Hellenistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Judaism) during the [Second Temple period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_period);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck200358–77-16][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman2003-13][^cite_note-Bsadducee-17] the [Karaites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaite_Judaism#Karaite_interpretations_of_the_Torah);[^cite_note-JEkaraites-18] and among segments of the modern non-Orthodox denominations. Some modern branches of Judaism, such as [Humanistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanistic_Judaism), may be considered secular or [nontheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nontheistic).[^cite_note-Mendes-Flohr2000-19][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKareshHurvitz2005221"Humanistic_Judaism"-20][^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] Today, the largest Jewish religious movements are [Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) (including the [Haredi](53835.md) and [Modern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox_Judaism) movements), [Conservative Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism), and [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism). Major sources of difference between these groups are their approaches to *Halakha* (Jewish law), [rabbinic authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_authority), and [Rabbinic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_literature), and the significance of the [State of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Israel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979-23][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael1984-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2007-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1] Orthodox Judaism maintains that the Torah and *Halakha* are explicitly divine in origin, eternal, and unalterable, and that they should be strictly followed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979218–270,_367–402-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael1984125–176-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003311–333-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Orthodox_Judaism"-29] Conservative and Reform Judaism are more liberal, with Conservative Judaism generally promoting a more traditionalist interpretation of Judaism's requirements than Reform Judaism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979317–346-30][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael198479–124-31][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003334–353-32][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Conservative_Judaism"-33] A typical Reform position is that *Halakha* should be viewed as a set of general guidelines rather than as a set of restrictions and obligations whose observance is required of all Jews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979156–185,_285–316-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael19841–78-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003291–310-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Reform_Judaism"-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKareshHurvitz2005419–422"Reform_Judaism"-38] Historically, rabbinical courts known as *batei din* (בָּתֵּי דִּין, 'houses of judgement'; sing. *[beit din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_din)*) enforced *Halakha*. *Batei din* are still existent, but the practice of Judaism is mostly voluntary.[^cite_note-Britannica_Online_Encyclopedia:_Bet_Din-39] Authority on theological and legal matters is not vested in any one person or organization but in the Jewish texts and the [rabbis](51273.md) and scholars who interpret them.

Judaism's texts, traditions, and values strongly influenced later religions and philosophies, including Christianity and [Islam](6037917.md); collectively known as [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions).[^cite_note-Islam,_Judaism,_and_Christianity:_Theological_and_Historical_Affiliations-40][^cite_note-The_Historical_Muhammad-41] By way of its relationship to Christianity, the dominant religion in the West, Judaism has had an indirect, yet significant role in the development of [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture).[^cite_note-Cambridge_University_Historical_Series-42][^cite_note-britannica.com-43]

In 2025, the world Jewish population was estimated at 14.8 million, although religious observance varies from strict to non-existent.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]

## Etymology

See also: [Ioudaios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioudaios)



*Maccabees* by [Wojciech Stattler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wojciech_Stattler) (1842)

The term *Judaism* derives from *Iudaismus*, a Latinized form of the Ancient Greek *[Ioudaismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioudaismos)* ([Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek_language): Ἰουδαϊσμός, from the verb ἰουδαΐζειν 'to side with or imitate the \[Judeans]').[^cite_note-LSJverb-46] Its ultimate source is the biblical "Yehudah" (יהוּדָה, *Yəhūda*), the Hebrew name for [Judah, son of Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_\(son_of_Jacob\)), and the namesake of the [tribe of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_of_Judah), the [region of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_\(region\)), and the [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah).[^cite_note-bibleinterp_mason3-47][^cite_note-askoxford8-48] The term *Ioudaismos* first appears in the [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek) book of [2 Maccabees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Maccabees) in the 2nd century [BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCE) (specifically 2 Maccabees 2\:21, 8\:1 and 14\:38).[^cite_note-\:0-49] In the context of the age and period, it meant "seeking or forming part of a cultural entity".[^cite_note-influence-50] It resembled its antonym *Hellenismos*, a word signifying submission to [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) cultural norms. The conflict between *Ioudaismos* and *Hellenismos* lay behind the [Maccabean Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabean_Revolt); hence, the term *Ioudaismos*.[^cite_note-influence-50]

[Rabbi](51273.md) [Shaye J. D. Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaye_J._D._Cohen) writes in his book *The Beginnings of Jewishness*:

> We are tempted, of course, to translate \[*Ioudaïsmós*] as "Judaism," but this translation is too narrow, because in this first occurrence of the term, *Ioudaïsmós* has not yet been reduced to the designation of a religion. It means rather "the aggregate of all those characteristics that makes Judaeans Judaean (or Jews Jewish)." Among these characteristics, to be sure, are practices and beliefs that we would today call "religious," but these practices and beliefs are not the sole content of the term. Thus *Ioudaïsmós* should be translated not as "Judaism" but as Judaeanness.[^cite_note-51]

[Daniel R. Schwartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_R._Schwartz), however, argues that "Judaism", especially in the context of the [books of the Maccabees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_the_Maccabees), refers to the religion, not the culture and politics of the Judean state. He believes it reflected the ideological divide between the [Pharisees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees) and [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees) and, implicitly, anti-Hasmonean and pro-Hasmonean factions in Judean society.[^cite_note-\:0-49]

According to the *Oxford English Dictionary*, the earliest citation of the term in English was in [Robert Fabyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Fabyan)'s 1516 book *The New Chronicles of England and France*, in which "Judaism" is described as "the profession or practice of the Jewish religion; the religious system or polity of the Jews".[^cite_note-52] "Judaism" as a direct translation of the Latin *Iudaismus* first appears in a Christian 1611 English translation of 2 Maccabees 2\:21: "Those that behaved themselves manfully to their honour for *Iudaisme*."[^cite_note-dictionary-53]

## Biblical and Talmudic narrative

### The covenant with Abraham in the book of Genesis

A large portion of the Hebrew Bible recounts the [Hebrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrews)' relationship with God from their earliest traditions through the [Second Temple period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_period) (i.e., until roughly 70 CE, when the [Temple was destroyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\))). [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham), initially called Abram (אַבְרָם, *Avram*), is presented as the ancestor of the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites), the descendants of [Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob)—whose name is changed to Israel (יִשְׂרָאֵל, *Yīsrāʾēl*) in [Genesis 32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genesis_32)\:29—and thus the Hebrews.[^cite_note-54] In the [patriarchal age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchal_age), God establishes a [covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covenant_\(biblical\)) with Abraham that includes the institution of circumcision (בְּרִית מִילָה, *[brit milah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_milah)*, 'covenant of circumcision') as a sign of that covenant, established when Abraham was 99 years old; the requirement to circumcise the males of his household is recorded in [Genesis 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genesis_17)\:10–14.[^cite_note-55] God changes Abram's name to Abraham in Genesis 17\:5 and Sarai's (שָׂרָי) name to [Sarah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah) (שָׂרָה).[^cite_note-56] Sarah is promised to bear a son in her old age, and that son, [Isaac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac) (יִצְחָק, *Yīṣḥāq*), will be the child of the covenant and Abraham's heir, whose descendants will inherit the land often called [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan).[^cite_note-57]

### The Torah, *Nevi'im*, and *Ketuvim*



A painting of [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses) decorates the [Dura-Europos synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dura-Europos_synagogue) dating from 244 CE

In the [book of Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Exodus), the second book of the Hebrew Bible, the descendants of Isaac's son Jacob were enslaved in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) during a period of harsh oppression. God, appearing to Moses in a divine vision through a [burning bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_bush) on [Mount Horeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Horeb), commands him to lead the Hebrews out of bondage. God inflicts [ten plagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_plagues) upon Egypt—such as the Nile turning to blood, swarms of locusts, and the death of the firstborn—to persuade [Pharaoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharaohs_in_the_Bible) to release the Hebrews. After the final plague, Pharaoh relents, and the Hebrews begin their escape, known as [the Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Exodus). They travel across the desert and arrive at [Mount Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Sinai_\(Bible\)), where God bestows the commandments, laws, and teachings that will define the moral and spiritual foundation of the Israelite community, as recounted in the subsequent chapters.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] These books, together with the *[Nevi'im](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevi'im)* and *[Ketuvim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketuvim)*, are known as Written Torah, as opposed to the Oral Torah, which refers to the Mishnah, Talmud, and [halakhic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakhic) *[Midrashim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrash)* (מִדְרָשִׁים, 'expositions'; sing. *Midrash*).[^cite_note-60] The *Nevi'im* consist of historical narratives and prophetic writings, focusing on the Israelites' settlements in Canaan. The *Ketuvim*, a diverse collection of books including the [book of Psalms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Psalms), [book of Proverbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Proverbs), and [book of Esther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Esther), covers poetic and prose philosophical writings that deviate from the more literalist style of the other books.[^cite_note-61]

### The Talmud

Rabbinic tradition holds that the details and interpretation of the Oral Torah were originally unwritten traditions based on the [Law given to Moses at Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_given_to_Moses_at_Sinai). However, as the [persecution of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews) increased in intensity and frequency and the details of the Oral Torah were in danger of being forgotten, [Judah ha-Nasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_ha-Nasi) compiled them into the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah), which was redacted c. 200 CE. The Talmud is a compilation of the Mishnah and [Gemara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gemara), rabbinic commentaries redacted over the next three centuries. The Gemara originated in two major centers of Jewish scholarship: [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmudic_academies_in_Syria_Palaestina) and [Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmudic_academies_in_Babylonia) ([Lower Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Mesopotamia)).[^cite_note-jewishencyclopedia.com-62] Correspondingly, two bodies of analysis developed, and two compilations of the Talmud were created. The older compilation is called the [Jerusalem Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Talmud). It was compiled sometime during the 4th century in Palestine.[^cite_note-jewishencyclopedia.com-62]

## History of Judaism

See also: [Jewish history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_history)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Jewish history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Jewish_history).

### Origins

Main article: [Origins of Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_Judaism)

Further information: [Yahwism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahwism), [Canaanite religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_religion), and [Ancient Semitic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Semitic_religion)

### Iron Age kingdoms

See also: [Ancient Israel and Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Israel_and_Judah) and [Solomon's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple)

"Ancient Judaism" redirects here. For the book, see [*Ancient Judaism* (book)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Judaism_\(book\)).



[Kingdoms of Israel and Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Israel_and_Judah) map [900 BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/900_BCE).

According to the Hebrew Bible, the [United Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Israel) was established under [Saul the King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saul_the_King) and continued under [King David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_David) and [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon), with its capital being [Jerusalem](16043.md). After Solomon's reign, the nation split into two kingdoms, the [Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)) in the north and the [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah) in the south. The Kingdom of Israel was destroyed around 720 BCE, when it was conquered by the [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire);[^cite_note-Broshi_2001_174-63] many people were taken captive from the capital [Samaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria_\(ancient_city\)) to [Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_\(region\)) and the [Khabur River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khabur_\(Euphrates\)) valley. The Kingdom of Judah continued as an independent state until it was conquered by [Nebuchadnezzar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebuchadnezzar_II) of the [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) in 586–87 BCE.

### Babylonian captivity, return, and Second Temple

See also: [Babylonian captivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_captivity), [Yehud (Persian province)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_\(Persian_province\)), [Second Temple Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_Judaism), [Hasmonean Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_Kingdom), [Iudaea Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iudaea_Province), and [First Jewish-Roman War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Jewish-Roman_War)

The Babylonians destroyed Jerusalem and the First Temple, forcing the Israelites into the Babylonian captivity in what is regarded as the first [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora). Many of the Israelites returned to their homeland—an event known as the [return to Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Return_to_Zion)—after the [fall of Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Babylon) was accomplished by the Persian [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) seventy years later. The [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) was constructed, and religious practices were resumed.

During the early years of the Second Temple, the highest religious authority was the [Great Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Assembly), led by [Ezra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra). Among other accomplishments of the Great Assembly, the last books of the Hebrew Bible were written at this time and [the canon was sealed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_of_the_Hebrew_Bible_canon). [Hellenistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Judaism) spread to [Ptolemaic Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Egypt) from the 3rd century BCE, and its creation sparked widespread controversy in Jewish communities, starting "conflicts within Jewish communities about accommodating the cultures of occupying powers."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck200358–77-16][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKareshHurvitz2005507-64] Later, during the [First Jewish–Roman War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_War) (66–73 CE), the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) sacked Jerusalem and destroyed the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple).

### Post-Temple Judaism

See also: [Bar Kokhba revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Kokhba_revolt) and [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora)

Later, Roman emperor [Hadrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian) built a pagan idol on the [Temple Mount](65719.md) and prohibited circumcision. These acts of ethnocide provoked the [Bar Kokhba revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Kokhba_revolt) (132–136 CE), after which the Romans banned [Torah study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah_study) and the celebration of [Jewish holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_holidays) and forcibly removed virtually all Jews from Judea. In 200 CE, however, Jews were granted Roman citizenship and Judaism was recognized as a *[religio licita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religio_licita)* (lit. 'licit religion'  or 'legitimate religion') until the rise of [Gnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosticism) and [early Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity) in the fourth century.

Following the destruction of Jerusalem and the expulsion of the Jews, Jewish worship stopped being centrally organized around the Temple; prayer took the place of sacrifice; worship was conducted within the Jewish communities of the diaspora; and the authority of [Chazal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chazal) (pronounced [\[χaˈzal\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Hebrew); חֲזַ״ל, a [Hebrew acronym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_acronym) for חֲכָמֵינוּ זִכְרוֹנָם לִבְרָכָה, *Ḥakhaméinu zikhronam livrakhah*, 'our Sages, of blessed memory') and rabbis who acted as teachers and leaders of individual communities was established.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck200378–92-12][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman2003-13]



The [Western Wall](75225.md) in [Jerusalem](16043.md) is a remnant of the wall encircling the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple). The [Temple Mount](65719.md) is the holiest site in Judaism.

#### Judaism in pre-Islamic Arabia

[Judaism in pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia) goes back to the pre-Christian period, and was concentrated in the northwest and south. In the fourth century, the ruling class of the [Himyarite Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite_Kingdom) of [pre-Islamic South Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia) converted to Judaism. This situation lasted until the early sixth century, when the [Aksumite invasion of Himyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksumite_invasion_of_Himyar), instigated by the massacre of [Najran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najran), led to a transition to [Christian domination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom).[^cite_note-65]

## Defining characteristics and principles of faith

Further information: [God in Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism)



[Kennicott Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennicott_Bible), a 1476 Spanish Tanakh

Unlike other ancient Near Eastern gods, the Jewish God is portrayed as unitary and solitary; consequently, the Jewish God's principal relationships are not with other gods, but with the world, and more specifically, with the people he created.[^cite_note-66] Judaism thus begins with [ethical monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethical_monotheism): the belief that God is one (הַשֵּׁם אֶחָד, *haššēm ʾeḥād*, '[HaShem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HaShem) \[is] one'), reflected by religious Jews' twice-daily recitation of *[Shema Yisrael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shema_Yisrael)* (שְׁמַע יִשְׂרָאֵל ה׳ אֱלֹקֵינוּ ה׳ אֶחָד, *šəmaʿ yiśrāʾēl YHWH ʾĕlōhê-nû YHWH ʾeḥād*, 'Listen, Yisra'el: HaShem \[is] our God; HaShem \[is] one'), and concerned with the actions of humankind.[^cite_note-google1-67] According to the Hebrew Bible, God promised Abraham that he would make of his offspring a great nation.[^cite_note-everlasting-68] Many generations later, he commanded the Israelites to love and worship only one God; that is, the Jewish nation is to reciprocate God's concern for the world.[^cite_note-deuteronomy-69] He also commanded the Jewish people to love one another; that is, Jews are to imitate God's love for people.[^cite_note-leviticus-70]

Thus, although there is an esoteric tradition in Judaism in [Kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah), Conservative rabbi and scholar [Max Kadushin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Kadushin) has characterized normative Judaism as "normal mysticism", because it involves everyday personal experiences of God through ways or modes that are common to all Jews.[^cite_note-publishing-71] This is played out through the observance of *Halakha* and given verbal expression in the *[Birkhot Ha-Mitzvot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_prayers_and_blessings)* (בִּרְכוֹת הַמּצְווֹת, 'Blessings \[of] the Mitzvot'), the short blessings recited every time a positive commandment is to be fulfilled:

> The ordinary, familiar, everyday things and occurrences we have, constitute occasions for the experience of God. Such things as one's daily sustenance, the very day itself, are felt as manifestations of God's loving-kindness, calling for the *Berakhot*. *[Kedushah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedusha)*, holiness, which is nothing else than the imitation of God, is concerned with daily conduct, with being gracious and merciful, with keeping oneself from defilement by idolatry, adultery, and the shedding of blood. The *Birkat Ha-Mitzwot* evokes the consciousness of holiness at a rabbinic rite, but the objects employed in the majority of these rites are non-holy and of general character, while the several holy objects are [non-theurgic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theurgy). And not only do ordinary things and occurrences bring with them the experience of God. Everything that happens to a man evokes that experience, evil as well as good, for a *Berakhah* is said also at evil tidings. Hence, although the experience of God is like none other, the *occasions* for experiencing Him, for having a consciousness of Him, are manifold, even if we consider only those that call for Berakot.[^cite_note-publishing2-72]

Whereas [Jewish philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_philosophers) often debate whether God is [immanent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanence) or [transcendent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendence_\(religion\)), and whether people have free will or their lives are determined, *Halakha* is a system through which Jews act to recognize God in the world. Ethical monotheism is central in all sacred or normative texts of Judaism. However, monotheism has not always been followed in practice. The Hebrew Bible records and repeatedly condemns the widespread worship of other gods in [ancient Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(united_monarchy\)).[^cite_note-mechon-mamre-73] In the [Greco-Roman era](252905.md), many different interpretations of monotheism existed in Judaism, including the interpretations that gave rise to Christianity.[^cite_note-The_Jewish_roots_of_Christological_monotheism:_papers_from_the_St._Andrews_conference_on_the_historical_origins_of_the_worship_of_Jesus-74]

Moreover, some have argued that Judaism is a non-creedal religion that does not require one to believe in God.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] For some, observance of *Halakha* is more important than belief in God *per se*.[^cite_note-jovanovich-77] The debate about whether one can speak of authentic or normative Judaism is not only a debate among religious Jews but also among historians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangton2011161–4-78]

### Core tenets

Main article: [Jewish principles of faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_principles_of_faith)

> The 13 principles of faith of Maimonides, summarized:[^cite_note-79]
>
> 1. There is a God
>
> 2. There is one God
>
> 3. God has no physical body
>
> 4. God is eternal
>
> 5. Only God may be worshipped
>
> 6. [Prophecy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_in_Judaism): God communicates with humans
>
> 7. [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses) was the greatest of the prophets
>
> 8. Torah comes from God
>
> 9. The Torah is the authentic word of God and may not be changed
>
> 10. God is aware of all our deeds
>
> 11. God rewards the righteous and punishes the wicked
>
> 12. The [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism) will come
>
> 13. The dead will be resurrected

Judaism does not possess fixed, universally binding articles of faith, in the same sense as those instituted by Christianity and Islam, though some are incorporated into the liturgy to a certain extent.[^cite_note-JEarticles-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011217–18"Dogma"-81] Scholars throughout [Jewish history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_history) have proposed numerous formulations of Judaism's core tenets, all of which have met with criticism.[^cite_note-JEarticles-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2007-25][^cite_note-montpelier-82] In the 12th century, [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides) developed his [13 principles of faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13_principles_of_faith), which is the most widely accepted formulation.[^cite_note-JEarticles-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2007-25] According to Maimonides, any Jew who rejects even one of these principles would be considered an [apostate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Judaism) and a [heretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy_in_Judaism).[^cite_note-mesora-83][^cite_note-Maimonides,_13_Principles_of_Faith-84] Jewish scholars have held points of view diverging in various ways from his principles.[^cite_note-learning-85][^cite_note-The_JPS_guide_to_Jewish_traditions-86] Thus, within [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism), only the first five principles are endorsed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1]

In the time of Maimonides, his list of tenets was criticized by [Hasdai Crescas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasdai_Crescas) and [Joseph Albo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Albo). Albo and [Abraham ben David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_ben_David) argued that his principles contained too many items that, while true, were not fundamentals of the faith.[^cite_note-JEarticles-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2007-25] Along these lines, the ancient Jewish historian [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) emphasized practice and observances rather than theology, associating apostasy with a failure to observe *Halakha* and maintaining that the requirements for [conversion to Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism) included [circumcision](8718425.md) and adherence to traditional customs. The 13 principles were largely ignored over the next few centuries.[^cite_note-medieval-87] Later, two poetic restatements of these principles, *[Ani Ma'amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ani_Ma'amin)* (אֲנִי מַאֲמִין, *ʾănî maʾămîn*, 'I believe') and *[Yigdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yigdal)* (יִגְדַּל, 'may \[God] be magnified'), became integrated into many Jewish liturgies,[^cite_note-JEarticles-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1][^cite_note-The_Thirteen_Principles_of_the_Jewish_Faith-88] leading to their eventual near-universal acceptance.[^cite_note-What_Do_Jews_Believe?-89][^cite_note-traditions-90]

The oldest instance of the [Karaite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaite_Judaism) movement's formulation of articles of faith is found in the work of the 12th-century figure [Judah Hadassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_Hadassi):

> 1. God is the Creator of all created beings;
>
> 2. He is [premundane](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/premundane) and has [no peer or associate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shituf);
>
> 3. The whole universe is created;
>
> 4. God called Moses and the other Prophets of the Biblical canon;
>
> 5. The [Law of Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Moses) alone is true;
>
> 6. To know the language of the Bible is a religious duty;
>
> 7. The [Temple at Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) is the palace of the world's Ruler;
>
> 8. Belief in [Resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_eschatology) contemporaneous with the [advent of the Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism);
>
> 9. [Final judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_judgment);
>
> 10. Retribution.

— Judah ben Elijah Hadassi, *Eshkol ha-Kofer*[^cite_note-JEarticles-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011217–18"Dogma"-81]

In modern times, Judaism lacks a centralized authority that dictates orthodoxies. Because of this, many variations on basic beliefs are considered within the scope of Judaism.[^cite_note-learning-85] Even so, all [Jewish religious movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements) are, to a greater or lesser extent, based on the principles of the Hebrew Bible and various commentaries, including the Talmud and Midrash. Judaism also universally recognizes the biblical [covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covenant_\(biblical\)) between God and the [patriarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchs_\(Bible\)) Abraham, as well as the additional aspects of the covenant revealed to [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses), who is considered Judaism's greatest [prophet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophet).[^cite_note-learning-85][^cite_note-ontario-91][^cite_note-How_Do_You_Know_the_Exodus_Really_Happened?-92] In the Mishnah, a core text of [Rabbinic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism), acceptance of the divine origins of this covenant is considered an essential aspect of Judaism, and those who reject the covenant forfeit their share in the [world to come](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_eschatology#World_to_come).[^cite_note-sacred-texts-93]

Establishing the core tenets of Judaism in the modern era is even more challenging, given the numerous and diverse contemporary Jewish religious movements. Even if restricting the problem to the most influential intellectual trends of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, the matter remains complicated. Thus, for instance, [Rav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rav) [Joseph B. Soloveitchik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_B._Soloveitchik)'s (associated with [Modern Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox_Judaism)) answer to modernity is constituted upon the identification of Judaism with following the *Halakha*, whereas its ultimate goal is to bring holiness down to the world. Rabbi [Mordecai Kaplan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mordecai_Kaplan), the originator of [Reconstructionist Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism), abandons the idea of religion for the sake of identifying [Judaism as a civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_as_a_civilization?action=edit\&redlink=1), and by means of the latter term and secular translation of the core ideas, he tries to embrace as many Jewish movements as possible. In turn, [Solomon Schechter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_Schechter)'s [Conservative Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism) was identical with the tradition understood as the interpretation of Torah, in itself being the history of the creative interpretation and reinterpretation of the Hebrew Bible and *Halakha*. Finally, [David Philipson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Philipson) draws the outlines of the Reform movement by opposing it to the strict and traditional rabbinical approach and thus comes to conclusions somewhat similar to that of the Conservative movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1][^cite_note-94]

## Religious texts



[Aleppo Codex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleppo_Codex), a Tanakh produced in [Tiberias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberias) in the 10th century

The following is a basic, structured list of the central works of Jewish practice and thought:

- The [Tanakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh) (תַּנַ״ךְ, *tanaḵ*), its translations, and its primary [exegeses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exegesis):&#x20;

  - The Tanakh itself:&#x20;

    - The [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) (תּוֹרָה, *Tôrâ*, lit. 'Instruction'  or 'Teaching')

    - The *[Nevi'im](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevi'im)* (נְבִיאִים, *Nĕbîʾîm*, 'Prophets')

    - The *[Ketuvim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketuvim)* (כְּתוּבִים, *Kĕtûvîm*, 'Writings')

  - The [Masorah](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Masorah) (מָסוֹרָה, 'tradition')

  - *[Targumim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targum)* (תַּרְגּוּמִים, 'translations')

  - Jewish biblical [exegesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exegesis) (also see *Midrash* below)

- Primarily legal works of the [Talmudic era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmudic_era) (i.e., classical [rabbinic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_literature))&#x20;

  - The [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) (מִשְׁנָה) and its commentaries

  - The [Tosefta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tosefta) (תוֹסֶפְתָּא, 'supplement') and the [minor tractates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_tractates)

  - The [Talmuds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud):&#x20;

    - The [Babylonian Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud#Babylonian_Talmud) (תַּלְמוּד בַּבְלִי, *Talmûd Bāblî*) and its commentaries

    - The [Jerusalem Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Talmud) (תַּלְמוּד יְרוּשַׁלְמִי, *Talmûd Yerûšalmî*; also known as תַּלְמוּד אֶרֶץ יִשְׂרָאֵל, *Talmûd ʾEreṣ Yiśrāʾēl*, 'Talmud \[of the] Land \[of] Israel') and its commentaries

- [Midrash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrash) (מִדְרָשׁ, 'textual interpretation'):&#x20;

  - *[Midrash Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrash_Halakha)* (מִדְרַשׁ הֲלָכָה)

  - *[Midrash Aggadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrash#Aggadic_midrashim)* (מִדְרַשׁ אַגָּדָה)

- [Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha) (הֲלָכָה, *Halākhāh*, 'Jewish law'):&#x20;

  - Codes of Jewish law and custom, such as:&#x20;

    - [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides)'s *[Mishneh Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishneh_Torah)* (מִשְׁנֵה תּוֹרָה, *Mišnê Tôrâ*, 'Repetition \[of the] Torah') and its commentaries

    - [Rabbi](51273.md) [Jacob ben Asher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_ben_Asher)'s *[Arba'ah Turim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arba'ah_Turim)* (אַרְבָּעָה טוּרִים, *ʾArbāʿāh Ṭurîm*, 'Four Rows'),

      [^cite_note-95]

      &#x20;as well as its commentaries

    - Rabbi [Joseph Karo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Karo)'s *[Shulchan Aruch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulchan_Aruch)* (שֻׁלְחָן עָרוּךְ, *Šulḥān ʿĀrûḵ*, 'Set Table') and its commentaries

  - The *[responsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsa#In_Judaism)* literature

- Other components of [Jewish thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_thought):&#x20;

  - Works of [Jewish philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_philosophy), such as those of [Jewish existentialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_existentialism) and [Holocaust theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_theology)

  - [Musar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musar_literature) (מוּסָר, *Mûsār*, 'ethics') and other works on [Jewish ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethics) and conduct

  - [Kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah) (קַבָּלָה, *Qabbālāh*, 'Reception')and other streams of [Jewish mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_mysticism)

  - Works of [Hasidic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_philosophy) (חֲסִידוּת, *Ḥasidût*, lit. 'piety'  or 'devoutness'), the foundation of [Hasidic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism)

- [Jewish prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_prayer) books, which vary across Jewish religious movements:&#x20;

  - *[Siddurim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siddur)* (סִדּוּרִים; sing., סִדּוּר, *siddûr*, 'order \[of prayers]'), which are used throughout most of the Jewish religious calendar

  - *[Machzorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machzor)* (מַחְזוֹרִים, *Maḥzôrîm*; sing., מַחְזוֹר, *maḥzôr*, lit. 'cycle'  or 'recurrence'), which are used only during the [High Holy Days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Holy_Days)

- Other works on the [Jewish liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_liturgy), including *[piyyutim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piyyut)* (פִּיּוּטִים, *piyyûṭîm*, 'liturgical poems'; sing., פִּיּוּט, *piyyûṭ*)



A man holds up a [Sephardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi)-style torah at the [Western Wall](75225.md), Jerusalem

### Legal literature

Main article: [Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha)

The basis of *halakha* and tradition is the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) (also known as the [Pentateuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentateuch) or the Five Books of Moses). According to rabbinic tradition, there are [613 commandments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/613_mitzvot) in the Torah. Some of these laws are directed only to men or to women, some only to the ancient priestly groups, the [Kohanim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohen) and [Leviyim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levite) (members of the tribe of [Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi)), some only to farmers within the Land of Israel. Many laws were only applicable when the [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) existed, and only 369 of these commandments are still applicable today.[^cite_note-96]

While there have been Jewish groups whose beliefs and practices were based on the written text of the Torah alone (e.g., the [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees), and the [Karaites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaite_Judaism)), most Jews follow the [oral law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_law#Oral_law_in_Judaism). These oral traditions were transmitted by the [Pharisee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees) school of thought in ancient Judaism and later recorded in writing and expanded upon by the rabbis.

According to Rabbinical Jewish tradition, God gave both the Written Law (the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah)) and the [Oral Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_Torah) to Moses on [Mount Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Mount_Sinai). The Oral law is the oral tradition as relayed by God to Moses and from him, transmitted and taught to the sages ([rabbinic](51273.md) leaders) of each subsequent generation.

For centuries, the Torah existed only as a written text transmitted alongside the oral tradition. Fearing that the oral teachings might be forgotten, Rabbi [Judah haNasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_haNasi) undertook the mission of consolidating the various opinions into one body of law which became known as the *Mishnah*.[^cite_note-97]

The Mishnah consists of 63 tractates that codify *halakha*, the basis of the Talmud. According to [Abraham ben David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_ben_David), the *[Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah)* was compiled by Rabbi [Judah haNasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_the_Prince) after the destruction of Jerusalem, in [anno mundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anno_mundi) 3949, which corresponds to 189 CE.[^cite_note-98]

Over the next four centuries, the Mishnah was discussed and debated in both the world's major Jewish communities (in Israel and [Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Iraq)). The commentaries from each of these communities were eventually compiled into the two Talmuds, the Jerusalem Talmud (*Talmud Yerushalmi*) and the Babylonian Talmud (*Talmud Bavli*). These have been further expounded upon by the commentaries of various Torah scholars throughout the ages.

In the text of the Torah, many words are left undefined, and many procedures are mentioned without explanation or instructions. Such phenomena are sometimes offered to support the view that the Written Law has always been transmitted alongside a parallel oral tradition, thereby suggesting that the reader is already familiar with the details from other, i.e., oral, sources.[^cite_note-99]

*Halakha*, the rabbinic Jewish way of life, is then based on a combined reading of the Torah and the oral tradition—the Mishnah, the halakhic Midrash, the Talmud, and its commentaries. The *halakha* has developed slowly, through a precedent-based system. The literature of questions to rabbis, and their considered answers, is referred to as [responsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsa) (Hebrew *Sheelot U-Teshuvot*). Over time, as practices develop, codes of *halakha* are written that are based on the responsa; the most important code, the [Shulchan Aruch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulchan_Aruch), largely determines Orthodox religious practice today.

### Jewish philosophy

Main article: [Jewish philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_philosophy)



Statue of [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides) in [Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain), Spain

Jewish philosophy refers to the conjunction between serious study of philosophy and Jewish theology. Major Jewish philosophers include [Philo of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philo), [Solomon ibn Gabirol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_ibn_Gabirol), [Saadia Gaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadia_Gaon), [Judah Halevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_Halevi), [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides), and [Gersonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gersonides). Major changes occurred in response to the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) (late 18th to early 19th century), leading to the emergence of post-Enlightenment Jewish philosophers. Modern Jewish philosophy encompasses both Orthodox and non-Orthodox perspectives. Notable among Orthodox Jewish philosophers are [Eliyahu Eliezer Dessler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliyahu_Eliezer_Dessler), [Joseph B. Soloveitchik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_B._Soloveitchik), and [Yitzchok Hutner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzchok_Hutner). Well-known non-Orthodox Jewish philosophers include [Martin Buber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Buber), [Franz Rosenzweig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Rosenzweig), [Mordecai Kaplan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mordecai_Kaplan), [Abraham Joshua Heschel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Joshua_Heschel), [Will Herberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_Herberg), and [Emmanuel Lévinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_L%C3%A9vinas).

### Rabbinic hermeneutics

> 13 Principles of Hermeneutics:
>
> 1. A law that operates under certain conditions will surely be operative in other situations where the same conditions are present in a more acute form
>
> 2. A law operating in one situation will also be operative in another situation if the text characterizes both situations in identical terms.
>
> 3. A law that clearly expresses the purpose it was meant to serve will also apply to other situations where the identical purpose may be served.
>
> 4. When illustrative particulars follow a general rule, only those particulars are to be embraced by it.
>
> 5. A law that begins with specifying particular cases, and then proceeds to an all-embracing generalization, is to be applied to particulars cases not specified but logically falling into the same generalization.
>
> 6. A law that begins with a generalization as to its intended applications, then continues with the specification of particular cases, and then concludes with a restatement of the generalization, can be applied only to the particular cases specified.
>
> 7. The rules about a generalization being followed or preceded by specifying particulars (rules 4 and 5) will not apply if it is apparent that the specification of the particular cases or the statement of the generalization is meant purely for achieving a greater clarity of language.
>
> 8. A particular case already covered in a generalization that is nevertheless treated separately suggests that the same particularized treatment be applied to all other cases that are covered in that generalization.
>
> 9. A penalty specified for a general category of wrongdoing is not to be automatically applied to a particular case that is withdrawn from the general rule to be specifically prohibited, but without any mention of the penalty.
>
> 10. A general prohibition followed by a specified penalty may be followed by a particular case, normally included in the generalization, with a modification in the penalty, either toward easing it or making it more severe.
>
> 11. A case logically falling into a general law but treated separately remains outside the provisions of the general law except in those instances where it is specifically included in them.
>
> 12. Obscurities in Biblical texts may be cleared up from the immediate context or from subsequently occurring passages
>
> 13. Contradictions in Biblical passages may be removed through the mediation of other passages.

— [R. Ishmael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbi_Ishmael)

[^cite_note-translated-100]

[Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) and many other Jews do not believe that the revealed [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) consists solely of its written contents, but of its interpretations as well. The study of Torah (in its widest sense, including both poetry, narrative, and law, and both the Hebrew Bible and the Talmud) is, in Judaism, itself a sacred act of central importance. For the sages of the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) and Talmud, and for their successors today, the study of Torah was therefore not merely a means to learn the contents of God's revelation, but an end in itself. According to the Talmud:

> These are the things for which a person enjoys the dividends in this world while the principal remains for the person to enjoy in the world to come; they are: honoring parents, loving deeds of kindness, and making peace between one person and another. But the study of the Torah is equal to them all. (Talmud Shabbat 127a).

In Judaism, "the study of [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) can be a means of experiencing God".[^cite_note-publishing3-101] Reflecting on the contribution of the [Amoraim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amoraim) and [Tanaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanaim) to contemporary Judaism, Professor Jacob Neusner observed:

> The rabbi's logical and rational inquiry is not mere logic-chopping. It is a most serious and substantive effort to locate in trivialities the fundamental principles of the revealed will of God to guide and sanctify the most specific and concrete actions in the workaday world. ... Here is the mystery of Talmudic Judaism: the alien and remote conviction that the intellect is an instrument not of unbelief and desacralization but of sanctification.[^cite_note-invitation-102]

To study the Written Torah and the Oral Torah in light of each other is thus also to study *how* to study the word of God.

In the study of Torah, the sages formulated and followed various [logical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic) and [hermeneutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermeneutics) principles. According to David Stern, all Rabbinic hermeneutics rest on two basic axioms:

> first, the belief in the omni-significance of Scripture, in the meaningfulness of its every word, letter, even (according to one famous report) scribal flourish; second, the claim of the essential unity of Scripture as the expression of the single divine will.[^cite_note-indeterminacy-103]

These two principles make possible a great variety of interpretations. According to the Talmud:

> A single verse has several meanings, but no two verses hold the same meaning. It was taught in the school of R. Ishmael: 'Behold, My word is like fire—declares the Lord—and like a hammer that shatters rock' (Jer 23\:29). Just as this hammer produces many sparks (when it strikes the rock), so a single verse has several meanings." (Talmud Sanhedrin 34a).

Observant Jews thus view the Torah as dynamic, because it contains within it a host of interpretations.[^cite_note-indeterminacy4-104][^cite_note-Bamidbar_Rabah-105]

According to Rabbinic tradition, all valid interpretations of the [written Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Written_Torah) were revealed to Moses at Sinai in oral form and handed down from teacher to pupil (the oral revelation is, in effect, coextensive with the Talmud itself). When different rabbis forwarded conflicting interpretations, they sometimes appealed to hermeneutic principles to legitimize their arguments; some rabbis claim that these principles were themselves revealed by God to Moses at Sinai.[^cite_note-indeterminacy5-106]

Thus, [Hillel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillel_the_Elder) called attention to seven commonly used hermeneutical principles in the interpretation of laws ([baraita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraita) at the beginning of [Sifra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sifra)); [R. Ishmael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._Ishmael), thirteen (baraita at the beginning of Sifra; this collection is largely an amplification of that of Hillel).[^cite_note-introduction-107] [Eliezer b. Jose ha-Gelili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliezer_b._Jose_ha-Gelili) listed 32, largely used for the exegesis of narrative elements of Torah. All the hermeneutic rules scattered through the [Talmudim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmudim) and [Midrashim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrashim) have been collected by [Malbim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malbim) in *Ayyelet ha-Shachar*, the introduction to his commentary on the Sifra. Nevertheless, R. Ishmael's 13 principles are perhaps the ones most widely known; they constitute an important, and one of Judaism's earliest, contributions to [logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic), [hermeneutics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermeneutics), and [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence).[^cite_note-introduction6-108] [Judah Hadassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_Hadassi) incorporated Ishmael's principles into Karaite Judaism in the 12th century.[^cite_note-introduction7-109] Today, R. Ishmael's 13 principles are incorporated into the Jewish prayer book to be read by observant Jews daily.[^cite_note-jerusalem-110][^cite_note-congregations-111][^cite_note-publications-112][^cite_note-publication-113]

## Jewish identity

### Distinction between Jews as a people and Judaism

According to [Daniel Boyarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Boyarin), the underlying distinction between religion and ethnicity is foreign to Judaism itself, and is one form of the dualism between spirit and flesh that has its origin in [Platonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato) philosophy and that permeated [Hellenistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Judaism).[^cite_note-A_radical_Jew:_Paul_and_the_politics_of_identity-114] Consequently, in his view, Judaism does not fit easily into conventional Western categories, such as religion, ethnicity, or culture. Boyarin suggests that this in part reflects the fact that much of Judaism's more than 3,000-year history predates the rise of Western culture and occurred outside the West (that is, Europe, particularly medieval and modern Europe). During this time, Jews experienced slavery, anarchic and theocratic self-government, conquest, occupation, and exile. In the Jewish diaspora, they were in contact with, and influenced by, ancient Egyptian, Babylonian, Persian, and Hellenic cultures, as well as modern movements such as the Enlightenment (see [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah)) and the rise of nationalism, which would bear fruit in the form of a Jewish state in their ancient homeland, the Land of Israel. Thus, Boyarin has argued that "Jewishness disrupts the very categories of identity, because it is not national, not genealogical, not religious, but all of these, in dialectical tension."[^cite_note-A_radical_Jew:_Paul_and_the_politics_of_identity10-115]

In contrast to this point of view, practices such as [Humanistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanistic_Judaism) reject the religious aspects of Judaism, while retaining certain cultural traditions.

### Who is a Jew?

Main article: [Who is a Jew?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_is_a_Jew%3F)

According to [Rabbinic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism), a Jew is anyone who was either born of a Jewish mother or who [converted to Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism) in accordance with *halakha*[^cite_note-116]. [Reconstructionist Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism) and the larger denominations of worldwide [Progressive Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism) (also known as Liberal or Reform Judaism) accept the child as Jewish if one of the parents is Jewish, if the parents raise the child with a Jewish identity, but not the smaller regional branches. All mainstream forms of Judaism today are open to sincere converts, although conversion has traditionally been discouraged since the time of the Talmud. The conversion process is evaluated by an authority, and the convert is examined on his or her sincerity and knowledge.[^cite_note-Who_is_a_Jew?-117] Converts are called "ben Abraham" or "bat Abraham", (son or daughter of Abraham). Conversions have on occasion been overturned. In 2008, Israel's highest religious court invalidated the conversion of 40,000 Jews, mostly from Russian immigrant families, even though they had been approved by an Orthodox rabbi.[^cite_note-118]

Rabbinical Judaism maintains that a Jew, whether by birth or conversion, is a Jew forever. Thus a Jew who claims to be an atheist or converts to another religion is still considered by traditional Judaism to be Jewish. According to some sources, the Reform movement has maintained that a Jew who has converted to another religion is no longer a Jew,[^cite_note-university-119] and the Israeli Government has also taken that stance after Supreme Court cases and statutes.[^cite_note-Law_of_Return_5710-1950-120] However, the Reform movement has indicated that this is not so cut and dried, and different situations call for consideration and differing actions. For example, Jews who have converted under duress may be permitted to return to Judaism "without any action on their part but their desire to rejoin the Jewish community" and "A proselyte who has become an apostate remains, nevertheless, a Jew".[^cite_note-Jacob,_Walter_(1987)._Contemporary_American_Reform_Responsa._Mars,_PA:_Publishers_Choice_Book_Mfg.-121]

[Karaite Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaite_Judaism) believes that Jewish identity can only be transmitted by patrilineal descent. Although a minority of modern Karaites believe that Jewish identity requires that both parents be Jewish, and not only the father. They argue that only patrilineal descent can transmit Jewish identity on the grounds that all descent in the Torah went according to the male line.[^cite_note-JEkaraites-18]

The question of what determines Jewish identity in the State of Israel was given new impetus when, in the 1950s, [David Ben-Gurion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ben-Gurion) requested opinions on *mihu Yehudi* ("Who is a Jew") from Jewish religious authorities and intellectuals worldwide to settle citizenship questions. This is still not settled, and occasionally resurfaces in [Israeli politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Israel).

Historical definitions of [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity) have traditionally been based on *halakhic* definitions of matrilineal descent, and *halakhic* conversions. Historical definitions of who is a Jew date back to the codification of the Oral Torah into the Babylonian Talmud, around 200 [CE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era). Interpretations of sections of the Tanakh, such as [Deuteronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Deuteronomy) 7\:1–5, by Jewish sages, are used as a warning against [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage_in_Judaism) between Jews and [Canaanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan#Canaanites) because "\[the non-Jewish husband] will cause your child to turn away from Me and they will worship the gods (i.e., idols) of others."[^cite_note-122] Leviticus 24 says that the son in a marriage between a Hebrew woman and an [Egyptian](31912046.md) man is "of the community of Israel."[^cite_note-123] This is complemented by Ezra 10, where Israelites returning from Babylon vow to put aside their [gentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentile) wives and their children.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] A popular theory is that the rape of Jewish women in captivity brought about the law of Jewish identity being inherited through the maternal line, although scholars challenge this theory citing the Talmudic establishment of the law from the pre-exile period.[^cite_note-Klein2016-127][^cite_note-Schott2010-128] Since the anti-religious *[Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah)* movement of the late 18th and 19th centuries, *halakhic* interpretations of Jewish identity have been challenged.[^cite_note-129]

### Jewish demographics

Main article: [Jewish population by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_by_country)

The total number of Jews worldwide is difficult to assess because the definition of "who is a Jew" is problematic; not all Jews identify themselves as Jewish, and some who identify as Jewish are not considered so by other Jews. According to the *Jewish Year Book* (1901), the global Jewish population in 1900 was around 11 million. The latest available data is from the World Jewish Population Survey of 2002 and the Jewish Year Calendar (2005). In 2002, according to the Jewish Population Survey, there were 13.3 million Jews around the world. The Jewish Year Calendar cites 14.6 million. It is 0.25% of world population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1]

Jewish population growth is currently near zero percent, with 0.3% growth from 2000 to 2001. The overall growth rate of [Jews in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Jews) is 1.7% annually, and is consistently growing through [natural population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_population_growth) and extensive immigration.[^cite_note-130] The [diaspora countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora), by contrast, have low Jewish birth rates, an increasingly elderly age composition, high rates of [interreligious marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage_in_Judaism) and a negative balance of people leaving Judaism versus those joining.[^cite_note-131]

In 2022, the world Jewish population was estimated at 15.2 million, with the majority living in one of two countries: Israel and the United States.[^cite_note-132] About 46.6% of all Jews resided in Israel (6.9 million) and another 6 million Jews resided in the United States, with most of the remainder living in Europe, and other groups spread throughout Canada, Latin America, Asia, Africa, and Australia.[^cite_note-133] Jewish demographics represent diverse historical and cultural trajectories.[^cite_note-134] [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews), [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews), Ethiopian Jews ([Beta Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Israel)), [Mizrahi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews), and [Romaniote Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews), may possess unique customs and practices.[^cite_note-135]

In Israel, the classification of Jewish observance into categories like [Haredi](53835.md), [Dati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionism), [Masorti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masortim), and [Hiloni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiloni) was developed by sociologists and researchers studying the religious and cultural landscape of Israeli society. These distinctions emerged from surveys and studies conducted by the Israel Central Bureau of Statistics and scholars such as Shmuel Sandler, who explored how religious practices varied among different segments of the Jewish population. The categories were created to better understand the range of religious adherence, from the ultra-Orthodox Haredim to the secular Hilonim, with Dati and Masorti representing intermediary groups.[^cite_note-136]

### 2020 population estimate



Global percentage of adherents by religion. Jews make up about 0.2% of the world's population.

[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-137]

According to Pew Research Center (2020), about 85% of the global Jewish population of 14,780,000 is concentrated in two countries: Israel and the United States, which are the only nations with Jewish populations exceeding one million. Jews constitute 0.2% of the world's population. Outside these two primary countries, the nations with the largest Jewish populations, in descending order, are France, Canada, the United Kingdom, Argentina, and Russia.[^cite_note-138] Even though Jewish birth rates are similar to the global average, the overall population has grown more slowly because it is older on average. By 2010, about one in three Jews worldwide was aged 50 or above, giving Jews the largest proportion of older adults among major religious groups.[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-137]

Jewish communities are highly concentrated geographically: 96% live in the ten countries with the largest Jewish populations, totaling 14.2 million people. About 500,000 Jews live elsewhere in the world.[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-137] Today, roughly 27,000 Jews reside in Muslim-majority countries,[^cite_note-139] mainly in Turkey (14,200),[^cite_note-140] Iran (9,100), and Azerbaijan (5,000). In the Arab world, the biggest Jewish populations are found in Morocco (2,000), Tunisia (1,000), and the United Arab Emirates (500).[^cite_note-141]

Regionally, the Jewish population increased in the [Middle East and North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_and_North_Africa) (up 18% to nearly 7 million), and grew slightly in Asia-Pacific (2%) and North America (1%). It declined in [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md) (down 37% to 50,000), Latin America–[Caribbean](18956035.md) (down 12% to 390,000), and Europe (down 8% to 1.3 million). Despite these shifts, Jews remained a small and generally stable share of regional populations.[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-137]

## Jewish religious movements

Main article: [Jewish religious movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements)



Sephardi-style Torah scroll



Ashkenazi-style Torah scroll

### Rabbinic Judaism

Rabbinic Judaism (or in some older sources, Rabbinism;[^cite_note-142] Hebrew: "Yahadut Rabanit" – יהדות רבנית) has been the mainstream form of Judaism since the 6th century CE, after the codification of the Talmud. It is characterised by the belief that the [Written Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Written_Torah) (Written Law) cannot be correctly interpreted without reference to the Oral Torah and the voluminous literature specifying what behavior is sanctioned by the Law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck200378–92-12][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman2003-13][^cite_note-Brabbinic-14]

The [Jewish Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Enlightenment) of the late 18th century resulted in the division of Western Jewry (primarily, the [Ashkenazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews), but also western part of [Sephardim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews) and [Italian rite Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Jews), a.k.a. *Italkim*, and Greek [Romaniote Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews)—both last groups are considered distinct from Ashkenazim and Sephardim) into religious movements or denominations, especially in North America and Anglophone countries. The main denominations today outside Israel (where the situation is rather different)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeshenLiebmanShokeid2017-143] are Orthodox, Conservative, and Reform. The notion "traditional Judaism" includes the Orthodox with Conservative[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2007-25] or solely the Orthodox Jews:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky197998–115-144][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1]



Two Haredi Jewish couples at a bus stop in Jerusalem



Hasids at front of [Belz Great Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belz_Great_Synagogue), Jerusalem

- [Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) holds that both the Written and Oral Torah were divinely revealed to [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses) and that the laws within it are binding and unchanging. Orthodox Jews generally consider commentaries on the *[Shulchan Aruch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulchan_Aruch)* (a condensed codification of *halakha* that largely favoured Sephardic traditions) to be the definitive codification of *halakha*. Orthodoxy places a high importance on [the 13 principles of Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13_Principles_of_Faith) as a definition of Jewish faith.

Orthodoxy is often divided into [Haredi Judaism](53835.md) and [Modern Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox_Judaism). Haredi is less accommodating to modernity and has less interest in non-Jewish disciplines, and it may be distinguished from [Modern Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox_Judaism) in practice by its styles of dress and more stringent practices. Subsets of Haredi Judaism along both ethnic and ideological lines include [Hardal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardal) ("Nationalist Haredi" within [Religious Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionism)); [Hasidic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism), which is rooted in the [Kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah) and distinguished by reliance on a [Rebbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebbe)

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Rebbe,_Hasidic"-145]

&#x20;or religious teacher; their traditionalist opponents the *[Misnagdim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misnagdim)* (also known as Lithuanian or *Lita'im*); and [Sephardic Haredi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Haredi) Judaism, which emerged among [Sephardic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic) and [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews) (Asian and North African) Jews in Israel.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979218–270,_367–402-26]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael1984125–176-27]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENadler1997-146]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003311–333-28]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Orthodox_Judaism"-29]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZohar2005-147]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESegal2008113–117-148]

&#x20;"Centrist" Orthodoxy ([Joseph B. Soloveitchik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_B._Soloveitchik)) is sometimes also distinguished.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESegal2008121–123-149]



Conservative women rabbis, Israel

- [Conservative Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism) (known as *Masorti Judaism* outside North America and Israel) is characterized by a commitment to traditional *halakha* and customs, including observance of [Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat) and [kashrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashrut), a deliberately non-fundamentalist teaching of Jewish principles of faith, a positive attitude toward modern culture, and an acceptance of both traditional rabbinic and modern scholarship when considering Jewish religious texts. Conservative Judaism teaches that *halakha* is not static, but has always developed in response to changing conditions. It holds that the Torah is a divine document written by prophets inspired by God and reflecting his will, but rejects the Orthodox position that it was dictated by God to Moses.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979317–346-30]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael198479–124-31]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGillman1993-150]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003334–353-32]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Conservative_Judaism"-33]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElazarGeffen2012-151]

  [^cite_note-Torah_MiSinai\:Conservative_Views-152]

  [^cite_note-Conservative_Judaism-153]

  &#x20;Conservative Judaism holds that the Oral Law is divine and normative, but holds that both the Written and Oral Law may be interpreted by the rabbis to reflect modern sensibilities and suit modern conditions.

- [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism), called Liberal or Progressive Judaism in many countries, defines Judaism in relatively universalist terms, rejects most of the ritual and ceremonial laws of the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) while observing moral laws, and emphasizes the ethical call of the [Prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevi'im). Reform Judaism has developed an egalitarian prayer service in the vernacular (along with Hebrew in many cases) and emphasizes personal connection to Jewish tradition.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979156–185,_285–316-34]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael19841–78-35]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer1988177–194-154]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003291–310-36]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Reform_Judaism"-37]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKareshHurvitz2005419–422"Reform_Judaism"-38]

- [Reconstructionist Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism), like Reform Judaism, does not hold that *halakha*, as such, requires observance, but unlike Reform, Reconstructionist thought emphasizes the role of the community in deciding what observances to follow. It is sometimes recognized as the fourth major stream of Judaism.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudavsky1979347–366-155]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaphael1984177–194-156]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWertheimer1993169-157]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Reconstructionism"-158]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes-Flohr2005-1]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKareshHurvitz2005416–418"Reconstructionist_Judaism"-159]

- [Jewish Renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Renewal) is a recent North American movement which focuses on spirituality and social justice but does not address issues of *halakha*. Men and women participate equally in prayer.

  [^cite_note-Magid2005-160]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESegal2008123–129-161]

- [Humanistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanistic_Judaism) is a small non-theistic movement centered in North America and Israel that emphasizes [Jewish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture) and history as the sources of Jewish identity.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKareshHurvitz2005221"Humanistic_Judaism"-20]

  [^cite_note-162]

- [Subbotniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subbotniks) (Sabbatarians) are a movement of Jews of Russian ethnic origin in the 18th–20th centuries, the majority of whom belonged to Rabbinic and Karaite Judaism.

  [^cite_note-163]

  [^cite_note-164]

  &#x20;Many settled in the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land) as part of the Zionist [First Aliyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Aliyah) to escape oppression in the Russian Empire and later mostly intermarried with other Jews, their descendants included [Alexander Zaïd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Za%C3%AFd), Major-General Alik Ron,

  [^cite_note-165]

  &#x20;and the mother of [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon).

  [^cite_note-166]

#### Sephardic and Mizrahi Judaism

See also: [Sephardic Haredim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Haredim) and [Sephardic law and customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_law_and_customs)



[El Ghriba synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Ghriba_synagogue) in [Djerba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djerba), Tunisia

While [traditions and customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minhag) vary between discrete communities, it can be said that [Sephardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi) (Iberian, for example, most [Jews from France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_France) and [the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews_in_the_Netherlands)) and [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews) (Oriental) Jewish communities do not generally adhere to the "movement" framework popular in and among [Ashkenazi Jewry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jew).[^cite_note-167] Historically, Sephardi and Mizrahi communities have eschewed denominations in favour of a "big tent" approach.[^cite_note-168] This is particularly the case in contemporary Israel, which is home to the largest communities of Sephardi and Mizrahi Jews in the world. (However, individual Sephardi and Mizrahi Jews or some their communities may be members of or attend synagogues that do adhere to one Ashkenazi-inflected movement or another.)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZohar2005-147] Among the pioneers of Reform Judaism in the 1820s there was the Sephardic congregation [Beth Elohim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahal_Kadosh_Beth_Elohim) in [Charleston, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer1988232–235-169] A part of the European Sephardim were also linked with the Judaic modernization.[^cite_note-170]

Sephardi and Mizrahi observance of Judaism tends toward the traditional (Orthodox) and [prayer rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusach_\(Jewish_custom\)) are reflective of this, with the text of each rite being largely unchanged since their respective inception. Observant Sephardim may follow the teachings of a particular rabbi or school of thought; for example, the Sephardic [Chief Rabbi of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Rabbinate_of_Israel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZohar2005-147][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeshenLiebmanShokeid2017Part_5_"The_Sephardic_Pattern"-171][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011166"Chief_Rabbinate"-172]
