#### Jewish movements in Israel

Main article: [Religion in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Israel)

In Israel, as in the West, Judaism is also divided into major Orthodox, Conservative and Reform traditions.[^cite_note-Tabory1990-173][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeshenLiebmanShokeid2017Ch._18_"Americans_in_the_Israeli_Reform_and_Conservative_Denominations"-174][^cite_note-Beit-Hallahmi-175] At the same time, for statistical and practical purposes, a different division of society is used there on the basis of a person's attitude to religion.

Most Jewish Israelis classify themselves as "[secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_secularism)" (*[hiloni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiloni)*), "traditional" (*[masorti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masortim)*), "religious" (*[dati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism)*) or "ultra-religious" (*[haredi](53835.md)*).[^cite_note-Beit-Hallahmi-175][^cite_note-Kedem-176] The term "secular" is more popular as a self-description among Israeli families of western (European) origin, whose Jewish identity may be a very powerful force in their lives, but who see it as largely independent of traditional religious belief and practice. This portion of the population largely ignores organized religious life, be it of the official Israeli rabbinate (Orthodox) or of the liberal movements common to diaspora Judaism (Reform, Conservative).

The term "traditional" (*masorti*) is most common as a self-description among Israeli families of "eastern" origin (i.e., the Middle East, Central Asia, and North Africa). This term, as commonly used, has nothing to do with Conservative Judaism, which also names itself "Masorti" outside North America. Only a few authors, like Elliot Nelson Dorff, consider the American Conservative (masorti) movement and Israeli masorti sector to be one and the same.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011350-177] There is a great deal of ambiguity in the ways "secular" and "traditional" are used in Israel: they often overlap, and they cover an extremely wide range in terms of worldview and practical religious observance. The term "Orthodox" is not popular in Israeli discourse, although the percentage of Jews who come under that category is far greater than in the Jewish diaspora. What would be called "Orthodox" in the diaspora includes what is commonly called *dati* (religious, including [religious zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionism)) or *haredi* (ultra-Orthodox) in Israel.[^cite_note-Beit-Hallahmi-175][^cite_note-Kedem-176] The former term includes what is called "religious Zionism" or the "National Orthodox" community, as well as what has become known over the past decade or so as *haredi-leumi* ([nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) *haredi*), or "Hardal", which combines a largely *haredi* lifestyle with nationalist ideology. (Some people, in [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish), also refer to observant Orthodox Jews as *[frum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frum)*, as opposed to *frei* (more liberal Jews)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeshenLiebmanShokeid2017Part_4_"Nationalist_Orthodoxy"-178]

### Karaites and Samaritans

[Karaite Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaite_Judaism) defines itself as the remnants of the non-Rabbinic Jewish sects of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) period, such as the [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees). The Karaites ("Scripturalists") accept only the Hebrew Bible and what they view as the [Peshat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshat) ("simple" meaning); they do not accept non-biblical writings as authoritative. Some European Karaites do not see themselves as part of the Jewish community at all, although most do.[^cite_note-JEkaraites-18]

The [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans), a very small community located entirely around [Mount Gerizim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Gerizim) in the [Nablus](233369.md)/[Shechem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shechem) region of the [West Bank](33209.md) and in [Holon](231871.md), near [Tel Aviv](31453.md) in Israel, regard themselves as the descendants of the Israelites of the Iron Age [kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)). Their religious practices are based on the literal text of the written [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) (Five Books of Moses), which they view as the only authoritative scripture (with a special regard also for the [Samaritan Book of Joshua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Joshua_\(Samaritan\))).



Beta Israeli [Kahen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahen) at the Western Wall

### Haymanot (Ethiopian Judaism)

See also: [Haymanot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haymanot) and [Beta Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Israel)

Haymanot (meaning "religion" in Ge'ez and Amharic) refers the Judaism practiced by Ethiopian Jews. This version of Judaism differs substantially from Rabbinic, Karaite, and Samaritan Judaisms, Ethiopian Jews having diverged from their coreligionists earlier. Sacred scriptures (the Orit) are written in Ge'ez, not Hebrew, and dietary laws are based strictly on the text of the Orit, without explication from ancillary commentaries. Holidays also differ, with some Rabbinic holidays not observed in Ethiopian Jewish communities, and some additional holidays, like [Sigd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigd).

### Noahide (*B'nei Noah* movement)

Further information: [Noahidism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noahidism)

[Noahidism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noahidism) is a [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements) [religious movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_religious_movement) based on the [Seven Laws of Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Laws_of_Noah) and their traditional interpretations within [Rabbinic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism). According to the *halakha*, non-Jews ([gentiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentiles)) are not obligated to [convert to Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism), but they are required to observe the Seven Laws of Noah to be assured of a place in the [world to come (olam ha-ba)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_eschatology#World_to_come), the final reward of the righteous. The divinely ordained penalty for violating any of the Laws of Noah is discussed in the Talmud, but in practical terms it is subject to the working legal system which is established by the society at large. Those who subscribe to the observance of the Noahide covenant are referred to as *B'nei Noach* (Hebrew: בני נח, 'Children of Noah') or *Noahides* ([/ˈnoʊ.ə.haɪds/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)). Supporting organizations have been established around the world over the past decades by both Noahides and Orthodox Jews.[^cite_note-179]

Historically, the Hebrew term *B'nei Noach* has applied to all non-Jews as descendants of Noah. However, nowadays it's primarily used to refer specifically to those non-Jews who observe the Seven Laws of Noah.

## Jewish observances

### Jewish ethics

Main article: [Jewish ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethics)

Jewish ethics may be guided by *[halakhic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha)* traditions, by [customs of etiquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_etiquette), by other moral principles, or by central Jewish virtues. Jewish ethical practice is typically understood to be marked by values such as justice, truth, peace, loving-kindness ([chesed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chesed)), compassion, humility, and self-respect. Specific Jewish ethical practices include practices of charity ([tzedakah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzedakah)) and refraining from negative speech ([lashon hara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lashon_hara)). Proper ethical practices regarding sexuality and many other issues are subjects of dispute among Jews.

### Prayers

Main article: [Jewish prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_prayer)



A Yemenite Jew at morning prayers, wearing a [kippah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kippah) skullcap, prayer shawl and [tefillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tefillin)

Traditionally, Jews recite prayers three times daily, [Shacharit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shacharit), [Mincha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mincha), and [Ma'ariv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ariv) with a fourth prayer, [Mussaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mussaf) added on [Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat) and [holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_holiday). At the heart of each service is the *[Amidah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amidah)* or *Shemoneh Esrei*. Another key prayer in many services is the declaration of faith, the *[Shema Yisrael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shema_Yisrael)* (or *Shema*). The *Shema* is the recitation of a verse from the Torah ([Deuteronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuteronomy) 6\:4): *Shema Yisrael Adonai Eloheinu Adonai Echad*—"Hear, O Israel! The Lord is our God! The Lord is One!"



An Israeli female soldier prays at the Western Wall

Most of the prayers in a traditional Jewish service can be recited in solitary prayer, although communal prayer is preferred. Communal prayer requires a [quorum](237014.md) of ten adult Jews, called a *[minyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minyan)*. In nearly all Orthodox and a few Conservative circles, only male Jews are counted toward a *minyan*; most Conservative Jews and members of other Jewish denominations count female Jews as well.

In addition to prayer services, observant traditional Jews recite [prayers and benedictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_prayers_and_blessings) throughout the day when [performing various acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_prayers_and_blessings#Everyday_prayers_and_blessings). Prayers are recited upon [waking up in the morning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modeh_ani), before eating or drinking different foods, [after eating a meal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkat_Hamazon), and so on.

The approach to prayer varies among the Jewish denominations. Differences can include the texts of prayers, the frequency of prayer, the number of prayers recited at various religious events, the use of musical instruments and choral music, and whether prayers are recited in the traditional liturgical languages or the vernacular. In general, Orthodox and Conservative congregations adhere most closely to tradition, and Reform and Reconstructionist synagogues are more likely to incorporate translations and contemporary writings in their services. Also, in most Conservative synagogues, and all Reform and Reconstructionist congregations, women participate in prayer services on an [equal basis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism) with men, including roles traditionally filled only by men, such as [reading from the Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah_reading). In addition, many Reform temples use musical accompaniment such as organs and mixed choirs.

### Religious clothing



Jewish boys wearing [tzitzit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzitzit) and [kippot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kippah) play soccer in Jerusalem



Men wearing [tallitot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallit) pray at the Western Wall

Further information: [Jewish religious clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_clothing), [kippah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kippah), [tzitzit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzitzit), and [tefillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tefillin)

A *[kippah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kippah)* (Hebrew: כִּפָּה, plural *kippot*; Yiddish: יאַרמלקע, *yarmulke*) is a slightly rounded brimless skullcap worn by many Jews while praying, eating, reciting blessings, or studying Jewish religious texts, and at all times by some Jewish men. In Orthodox communities, only men wear kippot; in non-Orthodox communities, some women also wear kippot. *Kippot* range in size from a small round beanie that covers only the back of the head to a large, snug cap that covers the whole crown.

*[Tzitzit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzitzit)* (Hebrew: צִיציִת) ([Ashkenazi pronunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Hebrew): *tzitzis*) are special knotted "fringes" or "tassels" found on the four corners of the *[tallit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallit)* (Hebrew: טַלִּית) (Ashkenazi pronunciation: *tallis*), or prayer [shawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawl). The *tallit* is worn by Jewish men and some Jewish women during the prayer service. Customs vary regarding when a Jew begins wearing a tallit. In the Sephardi community, boys wear a tallit from bar mitzvah age. In some Ashkenazi communities, it is customary to wear one only after marriage. A *tallit katan* (small tallit) is a fringed garment worn under the clothing throughout the day. In some Orthodox circles, the fringes are allowed to hang freely outside the clothing.

[Tefillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tefillin) (Hebrew: תְפִלִּין), known in English as phylacteries (from the Greek word φυλακτήριον, meaning *safeguard* or *amulet*), are two square leather boxes containing biblical verses, attached to the forehead and wound around the left arm by leather straps. They are worn during weekday morning prayer by observant Jewish men and some Jewish women.[^cite_note-publishers-180]

A *[kittel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kittel)* (Yiddish: קיטל), a white knee-length overgarment, is worn by prayer leaders and some observant traditional Jews on the [High Holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Holidays). It is traditional for the head of the household to wear a kittel at the Passover seder in some communities, and some grooms wear one under the wedding canopy. Jewish males are buried in a *tallit* and sometimes also a *kittel* which are part of the *[tachrichim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tachrichim)* (burial garments).

### Jewish holidays

Main article: [Jewish holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_holiday)

Jewish holidays are special days in the Jewish calendar, which celebrate moments in Jewish history, as well as central themes in the relationship between God and the world, such as [creation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creation_myth), [revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation), and [redemption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation).

#### Shabbat

Main article: [Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat)



Two braided Shabbat [challot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challah) placed under an embroidered [challah cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challah_cover) at the start of the Shabbat meal

*[Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat)*, the weekly day of rest lasting from shortly before sundown on Friday night to nightfall on Saturday night, commemorates God's day of rest after six days of creation. It plays a pivotal role in Jewish practice and is governed by a large corpus of religious law. At sundown on Friday, the woman of the house welcomes the Shabbat by lighting two or more candles and reciting a blessing. The evening meal begins with the [Kiddush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiddush), a blessing recited aloud over a cup of wine, and the Mohtzi, a blessing recited over the bread. It is customary to have [challah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challah), two braided loaves of bread, on the table. During Shabbat, Jews are forbidden to engage in any activity that falls under [39 categories of *melakhah*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/39_categories_of_activity_prohibited_on_Shabbat), translated literally as "work". In fact, the activities banned on the Sabbath are not "work" in the usual sense: They include such actions as lighting a fire, writing, using money and carrying in the public domain. The prohibition of lighting a fire has been extended in the modern era to driving a car, which involves burning fuel and using electricity.[^cite_note-JEsabbath-181]

#### Three pilgrimage festivals

Main article: [Shalosh regalim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalosh_regalim)

Jewish holy days (*chaggim*), celebrate landmark events in Jewish history, such as the [Exodus from Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_from_Egypt) and the giving of the Torah, and sometimes mark the change of seasons and transitions in the agricultural cycle. The three major festivals, Sukkot, Passover and Shavuot, are called "regalim" (derived from the Hebrew word "regel", or foot). On the three regalim, it was customary for the Israelites to make pilgrimages to Jerusalem to offer sacrifices in the Temple:

- 

  A [haggadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haggadah) used by the Jewish community of Cairo in Arabic

  &#x20;[Passover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover) (*Pesach*) is a week-long holiday beginning on the evening of the 14th day of [Nisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisan) (the first month in the Hebrew calendar), that commemorates the [Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Exodus) from Egypt. Outside Israel, Passover is celebrated for eight days. In ancient times, it coincided with the barley harvest. It is the only holiday that centers on home-service, the [Seder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover_Seder). [Leavened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leavening_agent) products ([chametz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chametz)) are removed from the house prior to the holiday and are not consumed throughout the week. Homes are thoroughly cleaned to ensure no bread or bread by-products remain, and a symbolic burning of the last vestiges of chametz is conducted on the morning of the Seder. [Matzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matzah) is eaten instead of bread.

- [Shavuot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shavuot) ("Pentecost" or "Feast of Weeks") celebrates the revelation of the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) to the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelite) on Mount Sinai. Also known as the Festival of Bikurim, or first fruits, it coincided in biblical times with the wheat harvest. Shavuot customs include all-night study marathons known as Tikkun Leil Shavuot, eating dairy foods (cheesecake and blintzes are special favorites), reading the Book of Ruth, decorating homes and synagogues with greenery, and wearing white clothing, symbolizing purity.

- 

  A [sukkah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukkah)

  &#x20;[Sukkot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukkot) ("Tabernacles" or "The Festival of Booths") commemorates the Israelites' forty years of wandering through the desert on their way to the Promised Land. It is celebrated through the construction of temporary booths called *sukkot* (sing. *[sukkah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukkah)*) that represent the temporary shelters of the Israelites during their wandering. It coincides with the fruit harvest and marks the end of the agricultural cycle. Jews around the world eat in *sukkot* for seven days and nights. Sukkot concludes with [Shemini Atzeret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shemini_Atzeret), where Jews begin to pray for rain and [Simchat Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simchat_Torah), "Rejoicing of the Torah", a holiday which marks reaching the end of the Torah reading cycle and beginning all over again. The occasion is celebrated with singing and dancing with the Torah scrolls. Shemini Atzeret and Simchat Torah are technically considered to be a separate holiday and not a part of Sukkot.



Jews in Mumbai break the Yom Kippur fast with [roti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roti) and [samosas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samosa)

#### High Holy Days

Main article: [High Holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Holidays)

The High Holidays (*Yamim Noraim* or "Days of Awe") revolve around judgment and forgiveness:

- [Rosh Hashanah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosh_Hashanah), (also *Yom Ha-Zikkaron* or "Day of Remembrance", and *Yom Teruah*, or "Day of the Sounding of the [Shofar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shofar)"). Rosh Hashanah is the Jewish New Year (literally, "head of the year"), although it falls on the first day of the seventh month of the [Hebrew calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_calendar), [Tishri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tishri). Rosh Hashanah marks the beginning of the 10-day period of atonement leading up to Yom Kippur, during which Jews are commanded to search their souls and make amends for sins committed, intentionally or not, throughout the year. Holiday customs include blowing the shofar, or ram's horn, in the synagogue, eating apples and honey, and saying blessings over a variety of symbolic foods, such as pomegranates.

- [Yom Kippur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur), ("Day of Atonement") is the holiest day of the Jewish year. It is a day of communal fasting and praying for forgiveness for one's sins. Observant Jews spend the entire day in the synagogue, sometimes with a short break in the afternoon, reciting prayers from a special holiday prayerbook called a "[Machzor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machzor)". Many non-religious Jews make a point of attending synagogue services and fasting on Yom Kippur. On the eve of Yom Kippur, before candles are lit, a prefast meal, the "[seuda mafseket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seuda_mafseket)", is eaten. Synagogue services on the eve of Yom Kippur begin with the Kol Nidre prayer. It is customary to wear white on Yom Kippur, especially for Kol Nidre, and leather shoes are not worn. The following day, prayers are held from morning to evening. The final prayer service, called "Ne'ilah", ends with a long blast of the shofar.

#### Purim

Main article: [Purim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purim)



Purim street scene in Jerusalem



Jewish personnel of the US Navy light candles on Hanukkah

[Purim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purim) (Hebrew: [פורים](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c9/He-Purim.ogg/He-Purim.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> *Pûrîm* "[lots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleromancy)") is a joyous Jewish holiday that commemorates the deliverance of the [Persian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Jews) from the plot of the evil [Haman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haman_\(Bible\)), who sought to [exterminate](12441.md) them, as recorded in the biblical [Book of Esther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Esther). It is characterized by public recitation of the Book of Esther, mutual gifts of food and drink, [charity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alms) to the poor, and a celebratory meal (Esther 9\:22). Other customs include drinking wine, eating special pastries called [hamantashen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamantash), dressing up in masks and costumes, and organizing carnivals and parties.

Purim has celebrated annually on the 14th of the Hebrew month of [Adar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adar), which occurs in February or March of the Gregorian calendar.

#### Hanukkah

Main article: [Hanukkah](7388.md)

[Hanukkah](7388.md) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): חֲנֻכָּה, "dedication") also known as the Festival of Lights, is an eight-day Jewish holiday that starts on the 25th day of [Kislev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kislev) ([Hebrew calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_calendar)). The festival is observed in Jewish homes by the kindling of lights on each of the festival's eight nights, one on the first night, two on the second night and so on.

The holiday was called Hanukkah (meaning "dedication") because it marks the re-dedication of the Temple after its desecration by [Antiochus IV Epiphanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_IV_Epiphanes). Spiritually, Hanukkah commemorates the "Miracle of the Oil". According to the Talmud, at the re-dedication of the [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) following the victory of the [Maccabees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabees) over the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire), there was only enough consecrated oil to fuel the eternal flame in the Temple for one day. Miraculously, the oil burned for eight days—which was the length of time it took to press, prepare and consecrate new oil.

Hanukkah is not mentioned in the Bible and was never considered a major holiday in Judaism, but it has become much more visible and widely celebrated in modern times, mainly because it falls around the same time as Christmas and has national Jewish overtones that have been emphasized since the establishment of the State of Israel.

#### Fast days

Main articles: [Tisha B'Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisha_B'Av), [Seventeenth of Tamuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventeenth_of_Tamuz), [10th of Tevet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_of_Tevet), and [Tzom Gedaliah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzom_Gedaliah)

[Tisha B'Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisha_B'Av) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): תשעה באב or ט׳ באב, "the Ninth of [Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Av_\(month\))") is a day of mourning and fasting commemorating the destruction of the [First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Temple) and [Second Temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple), and in later times, the [expulsion of the Jews from Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhambra_Decree).

There are three more minor Jewish fast days that commemorate various stages of the destruction of the Temples. They are the [17th Tamuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventeenth_of_Tamuz), the [10th of Tevet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_of_Tevet) and [Tzom Gedaliah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzom_Gedaliah) (the 3rd of Tishrei).

#### Israeli holidays

Main articles: [Yom Hashoah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Hashoah), [Yom Hazikaron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Hazikaron), and [Yom Ha'atzmaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Ha'atzmaut)

The modern holidays of [Yom Ha-shoah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Ha-shoah) (Holocaust Remembrance Day), [Yom Hazikaron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Hazikaron) (Israeli Memorial Day) and [Yom Ha'atzmaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Ha'atzmaut) (Israeli Independence Day) commemorate the horrors of the [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust), the fallen soldiers of Israel and victims of terrorism, and Israeli independence, respectively.

There are some who prefer to commemorate those who were killed in the Holocaust on the [10th of Tevet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenth_of_Tevet#Day_of_general_kaddish).



A man reads a torah using a [yad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yad)

### Torah readings

Main article: [Torah reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah_reading)

The core of festival and [Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat) prayer services is the public reading of the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah), along with connected readings from the other books of the Tanakh, called [Haftarah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haftarah). Over the course of a year, the whole Torah is read, with the cycle starting over in the autumn, on [Simchat Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simchat_Torah).

### Synagogues and religious buildings

Main article: [Synagogue](48559.md)



The [Sarajevo Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarajevo_Synagogue) in [Sarajevo](26786.md), Bosnia and Herzegovina



[Great Synagogue (Jerusalem)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Synagogue_\(Jerusalem\))

Synagogues are Jewish houses of prayer and study. They usually contain separate rooms for prayer (the main sanctuary), smaller rooms for study, and often an area for community or educational use. There is no set blueprint for synagogues and the architectural shapes and interior designs of synagogues vary greatly. The Reform movement mostly refer to their synagogues as temples. Some traditional features of a synagogue are:



[Congregation Emanu-El of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregation_Emanu-El_of_New_York)

- The [ark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ark_\(synagogue\)) (called *aron ha-kodesh* by [Ashkenazim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) and *hekhal* by [Sephardim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews)) where the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) scrolls are kept (the ark is often closed with an ornate curtain (*[parochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parochet)*) outside or inside the ark doors);

- The elevated reader's platform (called *[bimah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bema#Judaism)* by Ashkenazim and *tebah* by Sephardim), where the Torah is read (and services are conducted in Sephardi synagogues);

- The [eternal light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_lamp) (*ner tamid*), a continually lit lamp or lantern used as a reminder of the constantly lit [menorah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menorah_\(Temple\)) of the [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem)

- The pulpit, or *amud*, a lectern facing the Ark where the [hazzan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazzan) or prayer leader stands while praying.

In addition to synagogues, other buildings of significance in Judaism include [yeshivas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshiva), or institutions of Jewish learning, and [mikvahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikvah), which are ritual baths.

### Dietary laws: *kashrut*

Main article: [Kashrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashrut)

The Jewish dietary laws are known as *[kashrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashrut)*. Food prepared in accordance with them is termed [kosher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosher_foods), and food that is not kosher is also known as *treifah* or *treif*. People who observe these laws are colloquially said to be "keeping kosher".[^cite_note-JEdietary-182][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011212–14"Dietary_Laws"-183]

Many of the laws apply to animal-based foods. For example, to be considered kosher, mammals must have split [hooves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hooves) and [chew their cud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruminants). The pig is arguably the most well-known example of a non-kosher animal.[^cite_note-The_Kosher_Pig?-184] Although it has split hooves, it does not chew its cud. For seafood to be kosher, the animal must have [fins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fins) and [scales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scale_\(zoology\)). Certain types of seafood, such as [shellfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shellfish), [crustaceans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crustaceans), and eels, are therefore considered non-kosher. Concerning birds, a list of non-kosher species is given in the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah). The exact translations of many of the species have not survived, and some non-kosher birds' identities are no longer certain. However, traditions exist about the *kashrut* status of a few birds. For example, both chickens and [turkeys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkeys) are permitted in most communities. Other types of animals, such as [amphibians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibians), reptiles, and most insects, are prohibited altogether.[^cite_note-JEdietary-182]

In addition to the requirement that the species be considered kosher, meat and poultry (but not fish) must come from a healthy animal slaughtered in a process known as *[shechitah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shechitah)*. Without the proper [slaughtering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_slaughter) practices even an otherwise kosher animal will be rendered *treif*. The slaughtering process is intended to be quick and relatively painless to the animal. Forbidden parts of animals include the blood, some fats, and the area in and around the [sciatic nerve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sciatic_nerve).[^cite_note-JEdietary-182]

*Halakha* also forbids the consumption of meat and dairy products together. The waiting period between eating meat and eating dairy varies by the order in which they are consumed and by community and can extend for up to six hours. Based on the Biblical injunction against cooking a kid in its mother's milk, this rule is mostly derived from the Oral Torah, the Talmud and Rabbinic law. Chicken and other kosher birds are considered the same as meat under the laws of *kashrut*, but the prohibition is rabbinic, not biblical.[^cite_note-shulchan-185]

The use of [dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dishware), serving utensils, and ovens may make food *treif* that would otherwise be kosher. Utensils that have been used to prepare non-kosher food, or dishes that have held meat and are now used for dairy products, render the food *treif* under certain conditions.[^cite_note-JEdietary-182]

Furthermore, all Orthodox and some Conservative authorities forbid the consumption of processed grape products made by non-Jews, due to ancient [pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan) practices of using wine in rituals. Some Conservative authorities permit wine and grape juice made without rabbinic supervision.[^cite_note-rabbinicalassembly-186]

The Torah does not give specific reasons for most of the laws of *kashrut*. However, a number of explanations have been offered, including maintaining ritual purity, teaching impulse control, encouraging obedience to God, improving health, reducing [cruelty to animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruelty_to_animals) and preserving the distinctness of the Jewish community.[^cite_note-JEdietary-182] The various categories of dietary laws may have developed for different reasons, and some may exist for multiple reasons. For example, people are forbidden from consuming the blood of birds and mammals because, according to the Torah, this is where animal souls are contained. In contrast, the Torah forbids Israelites from eating non-kosher species because "they are unclean".[^cite_note-leviticus11-187] The [Kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah) describes sparks of holiness that are released by the act of eating kosher foods but are too tightly bound in non-kosher foods to be released by eating.[^cite_note-JEdietary-182]

Survival concerns supersede all the laws of *kashrut*, as they do for most *halakhot*.[^cite_note-jewishmag-188][^cite_note-biu-189]

### Laws of ritual purity

Main article: [Tumah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumah)

The Tanakh describes circumstances in which a person who is *tahor* or ritually pure may become *tamei* or ritually impure. Some of these circumstances are contact with human [corpses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpses) or [graves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grave_\(burial\)), seminal flux, vaginal flux, [menstruation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstruation), and contact with people who have become impure from any of these.[^cite_note-leviticus15-190][^cite_note-bamidbar-191] In Rabbinic Judaism, [Kohanim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohanim), members of the hereditary [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste) that served as priests in the time of the Temple, are mostly restricted from entering grave sites and touching dead bodies.[^cite_note-Torah_tidbits-192] During the Temple period, such priests (Kohanim) were required to eat their bread offering ([Terumah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terumah)) in a state of ritual purity, which laws eventually led to more rigid laws being enacted, such as [hand-washing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handwashing_in_Judaism) which became a requisite of all Jews before consuming ordinary bread.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusner1993-193][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFonrobert2005-194][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011"Purity_and_Unpurity,_Ritual"-195]

#### Family purity



18th-century circumcision chair [Museum of Jewish Art and History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Art_et_d'Histoire_du_Juda%C3%AFsme)

Main article: [Niddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niddah)

See also: [Women in Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Judaism)

An important subcategory of the ritual purity laws relates to the segregation of menstruating women. These laws are also known as *[niddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niddah)*, literally "separation", or family purity. Vital aspects of *halakha* for traditionally observant Jews, they are not usually followed by Jews in liberal denominations.[^cite_note-JEniddah-196]

Especially in [Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism), the Biblical laws are augmented by Rabbinical injunctions. For example, the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) mandates that a woman in her normal menstrual period must abstain from [sexual intercourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_intercourse) for seven days. A woman whose menstruation is prolonged must continue to abstain for seven more days after bleeding has stopped.[^cite_note-leviticus15-190] The Rabbis conflated ordinary *niddah* with this extended menstrual period, known in the Torah as *[zavah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zavah)*, and mandated that a woman may not have sexual intercourse with her husband from the time she begins her [menstrual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstrual_cycle) flow until seven days after it ends. In addition, [Rabbinical law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinical_law) forbids the husband from touching or sharing a bed with his wife during this period. Afterwards, purification can occur in a ritual bath called a [mikveh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikveh)[^cite_note-JEniddah-196]

Traditional [Ethiopian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Jews) keep menstruating women in separate huts and, similar to Karaite practice, do not allow menstruating women into their temples because of a temple's special sanctity. Emigration to Israel and the influence of other Jewish denominations have led to Ethiopian Jews adopting more normative Jewish practices.[^cite_note-Karaites-197][^cite_note-Women_and_water:_menstruation_in_Jewish_life_and_law-198]



Two boys wearing [tallit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallit) at a [bar mitzvah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_and_bat_mitzvah). The torah is visible in the foreground.

### Life-cycle events

Life-cycle events, or [rites of passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rites_of_passage), occur throughout a Jew's life that serves to strengthen Jewish identity and bind him/her to the entire community:

- *[Brit milah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_milah)* – Welcoming male babies into the covenant through the rite of [circumcision](8718425.md) on their eighth day of life. The baby boy is also given his Hebrew name in the ceremony. A naming ceremony intended as a parallel ritual for girls, named *[zeved habat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeved_habat)* or brit bat, enjoys limited popularity.

- [Bar mitzvah and Bat mitzvah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Mitzvah_/_Bat_Mitzvah) – This passage from childhood to adulthood takes place when a female Jew is twelve and a male Jew is thirteen years old among Orthodox and some Conservative congregations. In the Reform movement, both girls and boys have their bat/bar mitzvah at age thirteen. This is often commemorated by having the new adults, male only in the Orthodox tradition, lead the congregation in prayer and publicly read a "portion" of the Torah.

- [Marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_views_on_marriage) – Marriage is an extremely important lifecycle event and an ideal human state.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobs2003"Marriage"-199]

  &#x20;A wedding takes place under a *[chuppah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuppah)*, or wedding canopy, which symbolizes a happy house. At the end of the ceremony, the groom breaks a glass with his foot, symbolizing the continuous mourning for the destruction of the Temple, and the scattering of the Jewish people. An [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage_in_Judaism) is prohibited, except as within Reform Judaism:

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011381–2"Intermarriage"-200]



*Le Get* (The Divorce) by [Moshe Rynecki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moshe_Rynecki), c. 1930

- Divorce – Divorce is allowed in accordance with Halakha. The divorce ceremony involves the husband giving the short *[get](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Get_\(divorce_document\))* document written in Aramaic into the hand of the wife in rabbinical court, that is all. But, since the 11th century among the Ashkenazim and many Sephardim a divorce became prohibited against will of a wife, than a man had way for [polygamy](24475.md).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011216–7"Divorce"-201]

  &#x20;The *get* contains declaration: "You are hereby permitted to all men."



The Bereavement (Yahrtzeit) Hasidic *[tish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tish_\(Hasidic_celebration\))*, [Bnei Brak](314671.md), Israel

- [Death and Mourning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bereavement_in_Judaism) (*Avelut*) – The *Torah* requires burial as soon as possible, even for executed criminals.

  [^cite_note-202]

  &#x20;Judaism has a multi-staged [mourning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mourning) practice. The first stage is called the [shiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiva_\(Judaism\)) (literally "seven", observed for one week) during which it is traditional to sit at home and be comforted by friends and family, the second is the *shloshim* (observed for one month) and for those who have lost one of their parents, there is a third stage, *avelut yud bet chodesh*, which is observed for eleven months.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011205–6"Death"-203]

  &#x20;A [cremation](175142.md) within Orthodox Judaism permitted only by some leading rabbis in West Europe.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerlin2011193–4"Cremation"-204]

## Community leadership

### Classical priesthood



Jewish students with their teacher in [Samarkand](51491.md), Uzbekistan c. 1910.

The role of the priesthood in Judaism has significantly diminished since the destruction of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) in 70 CE when priests attended to the Temple and sacrifices. The priesthood is an inherited position, and although priests no longer have any but ceremonial duties, they are still honoured in many Jewish communities. Many Orthodox Jewish communities believe that they will be needed again for a future [Third Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Temple) and need to remain in readiness for future duty:

- [Kohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohen) (priest) – patrilineal descendant of [Aaron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron), brother of [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses). In the Temple, the *kohanim* were charged with performing the sacrifices. Today, a Kohen is the first one called up at the reading of the Torah, performs the [Priestly Blessing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priestly_Blessing), as well as complying with other unique laws and ceremonies, including the ceremony of redemption of the first-born.

- Levi ([Levite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levite)) – Patrilineal descendant of [Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi) the son of [Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob). In the [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem), the levites sang [Psalms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psalms), performed construction, maintenance, janitorial, and guard duties, assisted the priests, and sometimes interpreted the law and Temple ritual to the public. Today, a Levite is called up second to the reading of the Torah.

### Prayer leaders



[Magen David Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magen_David_Synagogue_\(Kolkata\)) in Kolkata, India

From the time of the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) and Talmud to the present, Judaism has required specialists or authorities for the practice of very few rituals or ceremonies. A Jew can fulfill most requirements for prayer by himself. Some activities—reading the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) and *[haftarah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haftarah)* (a supplementary portion from the Prophets or Writings), the prayer for mourners, the blessings for bridegroom and bride, the complete grace after meals—require a *[minyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minyan)*, the presence of ten Jews.

The most common professional clergy in a synagogue are:

- [Rabbi](51273.md) of a congregation – Jewish scholar who is charged with answering the legal questions of a congregation. This role requires ordination by the congregation's preferred authority (i.e., from a respected Orthodox rabbi or, if the congregation is Conservative or Reform, from academic seminaries). A congregation does not necessarily require a rabbi. Some congregations have a rabbi but also allow members of the congregation to act as *shatz* or *baal kriyah* (see below).&#x20;

  - Hassidic *[Rebbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebbe)* – rabbi who is the head of a [Hasidic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism) dynasty.

- [Hazzan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazzan) (note: the "h" denotes [voiceless pharyngeal fricative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voiceless_pharyngeal_fricative)) (cantor) – a trained vocalist who acts as *shatz*. Chosen for a good voice, knowledge of traditional tunes, understanding of the meaning of the prayers and sincerity in reciting them. A congregation does not need to have a dedicated hazzan.

Jewish prayer services do involve two specified roles, which are sometimes, but not always, filled by a rabbi or hazzan in many congregations. In other congregations these roles are filled on an ad-hoc basis by members of the congregation who lead portions of services on a rotating basis:

- Shaliach tzibur or *Shatz* (leader—literally "agent" or "representative"—of the congregation) leads those assembled in prayer and sometimes prays on behalf of the community. When a *shatz* recites a prayer on behalf of the congregation, he is *not* acting as an intermediary but rather as a facilitator. The entire congregation participates in the recital of such prayers by saying *amen* at their conclusion; it is with this act that the *shatz's* prayer becomes the prayer of the congregation. Any adult capable of reciting the prayers clearly may act as *shatz*. In Orthodox congregations and some Conservative congregations, only men can be prayer leaders, but all [Progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Judaism) communities now allow women to serve in this function.

- The Baal kriyah or *baal koreh* (master of the reading) reads the weekly [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) portion. The requirements for being the *baal kriyah* are the same as those for the *shatz*. These roles are not mutually exclusive. The same person is often qualified to fill more than one role and often does. Often there are several people capable of filling these roles and different services (or parts of services) will be led by each.

Many congregations, especially larger ones, also rely on a:

- [Gabbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabbai) (sexton) – Calls people up to the Torah, appoints the *shatz* for each prayer session if there is no standard *shatz*, and makes certain that the synagogue is kept clean and supplied.

The three preceding positions are usually voluntary and considered an honour. Since the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) large synagogues have often adopted the practice of hiring rabbis and hazzans to act as *shatz* and *baal kriyah*, and this is still typically the case in many Conservative and Reform congregations. However, in most Orthodox synagogues these positions are filled by laypeople on a rotating or ad-hoc basis. Although most congregations hire one or more Rabbis, the use of a professional hazzan is generally declining in American congregations, and the use of professionals for other offices is rarer still.



A [Yemeni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemenite_Jews) sofer writing a torah in the 1930s

### Specialized religious roles

- *[Dayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beth_din#Officers_of_a_beth_din)* (judge) – An ordained rabbi with special legal training who belongs to a *[beth din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beth_din)* (rabbinical court). In Israel, religious courts handle marriage and divorce cases, conversion and financial disputes in the Jewish community.

- [Mohel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohel) (circumciser) – An expert in the laws of circumcision who has received training from a previously qualified *mohel* and performs the *[brit milah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_milah)* (circumcision).

- [Shochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shechita) (ritual slaughterer) – For meat to be kosher, it must be slaughtered by a *shochet* who is an expert in the laws of kashrut and has been trained by another *shochet.*

- [Sofer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofer_\(scribe\)) (scribe) – [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) scrolls, *[tefillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tefillin)* (phylacteries), *[mezuzot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezuzah)* (scrolls put on doorposts), and *gittin* (bills of divorce) must be written by a *sofer* who is an expert in Hebrew calligraphy and has undergone rigorous training in the laws of writing sacred texts.

- [Rosh yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosh_yeshiva) – A Torah scholar who runs a [yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshiva).

- [Mashgiach/Mashgicha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashgiach_ruchani) of a yeshiva – Depending on which yeshiva, might either be the person responsible for ensuring attendance and proper conduct, or even supervise the emotional and spiritual welfare of the students and give lectures on [mussar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mussar_movement) (Jewish ethics).

- [Mashgiach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashgiach)/Mashgicha – Supervises manufacturers of kosher food, importers, caterers and restaurants to ensure that the food is kosher. Must be an expert in the laws of [kashrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashrut) and trained by a rabbi, if not a rabbi himself or herself.

### Historical Jewish groupings (to 1700)

Around the 1st century CE, there were several small Jewish sects: the [Pharisees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees), [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees), [Zealots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealots), [Essenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essenes), and [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity). After the destruction of the Second Temple in 70 CE, these sects vanished.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman2003-13][^cite_note-205] Christianity survived, but by breaking with Judaism and [becoming a separate religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schism_\(religion\)); the Pharisees survived but in the form of [Rabbinic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism) (today, known simply as "Judaism").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiffman2003-13] The Sadducees rejected the [divine inspiration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation) of the [Prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevi'im) and the [Writings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketuvim), relying only on the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) as divinely inspired. Consequently, a number of other core tenets of the Pharisees' belief system (which became the basis for modern Judaism), were also dismissed by the Sadducees. (The [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans) practiced a similar religion, which is traditionally considered separate from Judaism.)

Like the Sadducees who relied only on the Torah, some Jews in the 8th and 9th centuries rejected the authority and divine inspiration of the oral law as recorded in the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) (and developed by later rabbis in the two Talmuds), relying instead only upon the Tanakh. These included the [Isunians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isunians), the [Yudganites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yudganites?action=edit\&redlink=1), the [Malikites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malikites), and others. They soon developed oral traditions of their own, which differed from the rabbinic traditions, and eventually formed the [Karaite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaism) sect. Karaites exist in small numbers today, mostly living in Israel. Rabbinical and Karaite Jews each hold that the others are Jews, but that the other faith is erroneous.

Over a long time, Jews formed distinct ethnic groups in several different geographic areas—amongst others, the Ashkenazi Jews (of [central](5188.md) and Eastern Europe), the [Sephardi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) (of Spain, Portugal, and North Africa), the [Beta Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Israel) of Ethiopia, the [Yemenite Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemenite_Jews) from the southern tip of the Arabian Peninsula, and the [Malabari and Cochin Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochin_Jews) from Kerala. Many of these groups have developed differences in their prayers, traditions and accepted canons; however, these distinctions are mainly the result of their being formed at some cultural distance from normative (rabbinic) Judaism, rather than based on any doctrinal dispute.

### Persecutions

Main articles: [Persecution of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews), [Antisemitism](1078.md), and [History of antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism)

[Antisemitism](1078.md) arose during the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), in the form of persecutions, [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom), [forced conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_conversion), expulsions, social restrictions and [ghettoization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto).

This was different in quality from the repressions of Jews which had occurred in ancient times. Ancient repressions were politically motivated and Jews were treated the same as members of other ethnic groups. With the rise of the Churches, the main motive for attacks on Jews changed from politics to religion and the religious motive for such attacks was specifically derived from Christian views about Jews and Judaism.[^cite_note-History,_religion,_and_antisemitism-206] During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), Jewish people who lived under Muslim rule generally experienced tolerance and integration,[^cite_note-Cohen,_Mark_R_1991-207] but there were occasional outbreaks of violence like [Almohad's persecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohads#Status_of_non-Muslims).[^cite_note-ugr-208]

### Hasidism

Main article: [Hasidic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism)

Hasidic Judaism was founded by [Yisroel ben Eliezer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yisroel_ben_Eliezer_\(The_Baal_Shem_Tov\)) (1700–1760), also known as the *Ba'al Shem Tov* (or *Besht*). It originated in a time of persecution of the Jewish people when European Jews had turned inward to Talmud study; many felt that most expressions of Jewish life had become too "academic", and that they no longer had any emphasis on spirituality or joy. Its adherents favoured small and informal gatherings called [Shtiebel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shtiebel), which, in contrast to a traditional synagogue, could be used both as a place of worship and for celebrations involving dancing, eating, and socializing.[^cite_note-209] Ba'al Shem Tov's disciples attracted many followers; they themselves established numerous Hasidic sects across Europe. Unlike other religions, which typically expanded through word of mouth or by use of print, Hasidism spread largely owing to [Tzadiks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzadik), who used their influence to encourage others to follow the movement. Hasidism appealed to many Europeans because it was easy to learn, did not require full immediate commitment, and presented a compelling spectacle.[^cite_note-210] Hasidic Judaism eventually became the way of life for many Jews in Eastern Europe. Waves of Jewish immigration in the 1880s carried it to the United States. The movement itself claims to be nothing new, but a *refreshment* of original Judaism. As some have put it: *"they merely re-emphasized that which the generations had lost"*. Nevertheless, early on there was a serious schism between Hasidic and non-Hasidic Jews. European Jews who rejected the Hasidic movement were dubbed by the Hasidim as [Misnagdim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misnagdim), (lit. 'opponents'). Some of the reasons for the rejection of Hasidic Judaism were the exuberance of Hasidic worship, its deviation from tradition in ascribing infallibility and miracles to their leaders, and the concern that it might become a messianic sect. Over time differences between the Hasidim and their opponents have slowly diminished and both groups are now considered part of Haredi Judaism.

### The Enlightenment and new religious movements

Main articles: [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah) and [Jewish religious movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements)

In the late 18th century CE, Europe was swept by a group of intellectual, social and political movements known as the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment). The Enlightenment led to reductions in the European laws that prohibited Jews to interact with the wider secular world, thus allowing Jews access to secular education and experience. A parallel Jewish movement, [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah) or the "Jewish Enlightenment", began, especially in Central Europe and Western Europe, in response to both the Enlightenment and these new freedoms. It placed an emphasis on integration with secular society and a pursuit of non-religious knowledge through reason. With the promise of political emancipation, many Jews saw no reason to continue to observe *halakha* and increasing numbers of Jews assimilated into Christian Europe. Modern religious movements of Judaism all formed in reaction to this trend.

In Central Europe, followed by Great Britain and the United States, [Reform (or Liberal) Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism) developed, relaxing legal obligations (especially those that limited Jewish relations with non-Jews), emulating [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) decorum in prayer, and emphasizing the ethical values of Judaism's Prophetic tradition. [Modern Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox_Judaism) developed in reaction to Reform Judaism, by leaders who argued that Jews could participate in public life as citizens equal to Christians while maintaining the observance of *halakha*. Meanwhile, in the United States, wealthy Reform Jews helped European scholars, who were Orthodox in practice but critical (and skeptical) in their study of the Bible and Talmud, to establish a seminary to train rabbis for immigrants from Eastern Europe. These left-wing Orthodox rabbis were joined by right-wing Reform rabbis who felt that *halakha* should not be entirely abandoned, to form the [Conservative movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism). Orthodox Jews who opposed the Haskalah formed [Haredi Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haredi_Orthodox_Judaism). After massive movements of Jews following [The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) and the creation of the state of Israel, these movements have competed for followers from among traditional Jews in or from other countries.

### Spectrum of observance



1889 [siddur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siddur) published in Hebrew and [Marathi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_language) for use by the [Bene Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Israel) community

Jewish religious practice varies widely through all levels of observance. According to the 2001 edition of the [National Jewish Population Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Jewish_Population_Survey), in the United States' Jewish community—the world's second largest—4.3 million Jews out of 5.1 million had some sort of connection to the religion.[^cite_note-211] Of that population of connected Jews, 80% participated in some sort of Jewish religious observance, but only 48% belonged to a congregation, and fewer than 16% attend regularly.[^cite_note-harrisinteractive-212]

## Judaism and ecology

|  | It has been suggested that this section be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article titled *[Judaism and environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_and_environmentalism)*. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Judaism#Split_proposed)) *(April 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

Ecological concerns are deeply rooted in the Jewish tradition. The natural world plays a central role in Jewish law, literature, liturgy, and other practices. In [Jewish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_law) ([halakhah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakhah)), ecological concerns are reflected in several instances. These include, the Biblical protection for fruit trees, rules in the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) against harming the public domain, [Talmudic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud) debate over noise and smoke damages, and contemporary responsa on [agricultural pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_pollution).[^cite_note-IN-213] The rule of *tza'ar ba'alei hayyim* is a restriction on cruelty to animals.[^cite_note-214]

Although the Bible and [rabbinic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism) tradition have put Judaism on an anthropocentric path, creation-centered or eco-centric interpretations of Judaism can also be found throughout [Jewish history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_history).[^cite_note-215] Many theologians regard the land as a primary partner of Jewish covenant, and Judaism, especially the practices described in the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah), may be regarded as the expression of a fully indigenous, earth-centered tradition.[^cite_note-216]

Since the 1970s, hundreds of articles and books have been written on the topic of [Judaism and environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_and_environmentalism), and the moral obligation to care for God's Earth and its creatures. The article "Judaism and the Ecological Crisis"[^cite_note-217] and Dr. Eilon Schwartz's "Bal Tashchit: A Jewish Environmental Precept" note about the Jewish concept of Bal Tashchit, which prohibits unnecessary waste and encourages the sustainable use of resources.[^cite_note-218] Dr. Hava Tirosh-Samuelson, in a 2001 essay titled "Nature in the Sources of Judaism", notes how a Jewish perspective on nature is rooted in the belief that the universe is the creation of God.[^cite_note-219]

Scores of books have been published on Jewish teachings and environmental stewardship. Among them are "Eco Bible: Volume 1: An Ecological Commentary on Genesis and Exodus", and "Eco Bible: Volume 2: An Ecological Commentary on Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy".[^cite_note-220]

## Judaism and other religions

### Christianity and Judaism

Main article: [Christianity and Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_Judaism)

See also: [Christianity and antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_antisemitism) and [Christian–Jewish reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian%E2%80%93Jewish_reconciliation)



The 12th-century [Synagogue of Santa María la Blanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synagogue_of_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_la_Blanca) in [Toledo, Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo,_Spain), was converted to a church shortly after anti-Jewish [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) in 1391

Christianity was [originally a sect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christianity) of Second Temple Judaism, but the two religions [diverged in the first century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_of_early_Christianity_and_Judaism).[^cite_note-Hurtado_2005-221][^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] The differences between Christianity and Judaism originally centered on whether Jesus was the Jewish Messiah, but eventually became irreconcilable. Major differences between the two faiths include the nature of the Messiah, of [atonement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atonement_in_Judaism) and [sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_views_on_sin), the status of God's commandments to Israel, and perhaps most significantly, of the [nature of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) himself. Due to these differences, Judaism traditionally regards Christianity as *[shituf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shituf)* (שִׁתּוּף, 'association'), or worship of the God of Israel in an incompletely monotheistic manner (e.g., deifying Jesus in addition to the one God). Christianity has traditionally regarded Judaism as [obsolete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersessionism) with the invention of Christianity and Jews as a people replaced by the Church. However, a Christian belief in [dual-covenant theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-covenant_theology) emerged as a phenomenon following Christian reflection on how the religion's theology influenced [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) and [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism).[^cite_note-224]

&#x20;Since the time of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity_during_the_Middle_Ages), the [Catholic Church](606848.md) upheld the *[Constitutio pro Judæis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sicut_Judaeis)* (Formal Statement on the Jews), which stated:&#x20;

> We decree that no Christian shall use violence to force them to be baptized, so long as they are unwilling and refuse.…Without the judgment of the political authority of the land, no Christian shall presume to wound them or kill them or rob them of their money or change the good customs that they have thus far enjoyed in the place where they live."[^cite_note-BaskinSeeskin2010-225]

Until [Jewish emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_emancipation) in the late 18th and the 19th century, Jews in Christian lands were subject to humiliating legal restrictions and limitations. They included provisions requiring Jews to wear specific and identifying clothing such as the [Jewish hat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_hat) and the [yellow badge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_badge), restricting Jews to certain cities and towns or in certain parts of towns ([ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ghettos_in_Europe)), and forbidding Jews to enter certain trades (e.g., selling new clothes in medieval Sweden). Disabilities also included special taxes levied on Jews, exclusion from public life, restraints on the performance of religious ceremonies, and linguistic censorship. Some countries went even further and completely expelled Jews—for example, the English [Edict of Expulsion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Expulsion) in 1290 (Jews were readmitted in 1655) and [expulsion of the Jews from Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Jews_from_Spain) in 1492 (who were readmitted in 1868). The first Jewish settlers in North America arrived in the Dutch colony of [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam) in 1654; they were forbidden to hold public office, open a retail shop, or establish a synagogue. When the colony was seized by the British in 1664, Jewish rights remained unchanged; by 1671, [Asser Levy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asser_Levy) was the first Jew to serve on a jury in North America.[^cite_note-gotham-226] In 1791, [Revolutionary France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) was the first country to abolish disabilities altogether, followed by [Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussia) in 1848. [Emancipation of the Jews in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_of_the_Jews_in_the_United_Kingdom) was achieved in 1858 after an almost 30-year struggle championed by [Isaac Lyon Goldsmid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Lyon_Goldsmid),[^cite_note-227] with Jews given the right to sit in parliament with the passing of the [Jews Relief Act 1858](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_Relief_Act_1858). The newly created [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) in 1871 abolished Jewish disabilities in Germany, which were reinstated in the [Nuremberg Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_Laws) in 1935.

Jewish life in Christian lands was marred by [blood libels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_libel), expulsions, [forced conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_conversion), and massacres. Religious prejudice fueled hostility against Jews in Europe. Christian rhetoric and antipathy towards Jews developed in the [early years of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Age) and was reinforced by ever-increasing anti-Jewish measures over the ensuing centuries. The action taken by Christians against Jews included acts of violence and murder, culminating in the Holocaust.[^cite_note-HarriesAfter-228]<sup>: 21 </sup>[^cite_note-Kung-229]<sup>: 169 </sup>[^cite_note-Dawidowicz-230] These attitudes were reinforced by Christian preaching, in art and popular teaching for two millennia which expressed contempt for Jews,[^cite_note-JCPSHorst-231] as well as statutes which were designed to humiliate and stigmatise Jews, such as those of the *[Judensau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judensau)* motif. The [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) was known for its [persecution of Christian churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirchenkampf); many of them, such as the Protestant [Confessing Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessing_Church) and the Catholic Church,[^cite_note-232] as well as [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers) and [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses), aided and rescued Jews who were being targeted by the antireligious régime.[^cite_note-Gottfried2001-233]

The attitude of Christians and Christian churches toward the Jewish people and Judaism has changed in a mostly positive direction since [World War II](32927.md). Pope [John Paul II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_II) and the Catholic Church have "upheld the Church's acceptance of the continuing and permanent [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_as_the_chosen_people) of the Jewish people" as well as a [reaffirmation of the covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-covenant_theology) between [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity) and the Jews.[^cite_note-Wigoder1988-234] In December 2015, the [Vatican](13393.md) released a 10,000-word document that, among other things, stated that Catholics should work with Jews to fight antisemitism.[^cite_note-news.va-235]

In [continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe), Judaism is heavily associated with and most often thought of as [Orthodox Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism).[^cite_note-236]

### Islam and Judaism

Main article: [Islam and Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_Judaism)



Muslim women in the [mellah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mellah) of [Essaouira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essaouira)



The bimah of the [Ben Ezra Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Ezra_Synagogue) in Cairo, Egypt

Both Judaism and [Islam](6037917.md) trace their origins to the patriarch Abraham, and they are therefore considered [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions). In both Jewish and [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) tradition, the Jewish and [Arab peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_peoples) are descended from the two sons of Abraham—[Isaac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac) and [Ishmael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmael), respectively. While both religions are [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism) and share many commonalities, they differ based on the fact that Jews do not consider [Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam) or [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) to be prophets, among many other reasons. The adherents of the religions have interacted with each other since the 7th century, when Islam originated and spread in the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md). The period under the [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) and the [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid) caliphates between 712 and 1066 has been called the [Golden age of Jewish culture in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_age_of_Jewish_culture_in_Spain). Non-Muslim monotheists living in these countries, including Jews, were known as *[dhimmis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmis)*. Dhimmis were allowed to practice their own religions and administer their own internal affairs, but they were subject to certain restrictions that were not imposed on Muslims.[^cite_note-Lewis-84-237] For example, they had to pay the [jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya), a per capita tax imposed on free adult non-Muslim males,[^cite_note-Lewis-84-237] and they were also forbidden to bear arms or testify in court cases involving Muslims.[^cite_note-lewis14-238] Many of the laws regarding dhimmis were highly symbolic. For example, dhimmis in some countries were required to wear [distinctive clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_badge), a practice not found in either the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) or the [hadiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadiths) but invented in [early medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages) [Baghdad](4492.md) and inconsistently enforced.[^cite_note-lewis15-239] Jews in Muslim countries were not entirely free from persecution—for example, many were killed, exiled or forcibly converted in the 12th century, in [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia), and by the rulers of the [Almohad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad) dynasty in North Africa and [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus),[^cite_note-stillman-240] as well as by the Zaydi imams of Yemen in the 17th century (see [Mawza Exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mawza_Exile)). At times, Jews were also restricted in their choice of residence—in Morocco, for example, Jews were confined to walled quarters ([mellahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mellah)) beginning in the 15th century and increasingly since the early 19th century.[^cite_note-lewis16-241]

In the mid-20th century, [Jews were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_Arab_and_Muslim_countries) from nearly all of the Arab countries.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] Most have chosen to live in Israel. Today, antisemitic themes including [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) have become commonplace in the propaganda of Islamic movements such as [Hizbullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizbullah) and [Hamas](13913.md), in the pronouncements of various agencies of the Islamic Republic of Iran, and even in the newspapers and other publications of [Refah Partisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refah_Partisi).[^cite_note-Lewis_MEQ-244]

### Syncretic movements incorporating Judaism

Some movements in other religions include elements of Judaism. Among Christianity, there are a number of denominations of ancient and contemporary [Judaizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaizers). The most well-known of these is [Messianic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messianic_Judaism), a religious movement, which arose in the 1960s,[^cite_note-Feher1998p140-245][^cite_note-Ariel2006p191b-246][^cite_note-Ariel2006p194a-247][^cite_note-Meltonp373a-248] In this, elements of the messianic traditions in Judaism,[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] are incorporated in, and melded with the [tenets of Christianity](5211.md#Beliefs).[^cite_note-Meltonp373a-248][^cite_note-Ariel2006p191a-251][^cite_note-Ariel2006p194b-252][^cite_note-Sherbok_179-253][^cite_note-Ariel2000p223-254] The movement generally states that Jesus is the Jewish Messiah, that he is one of the [Three Divine Persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity),[^cite_note-UMJC-3-255][^cite_note-Trinitarianism-256] and that [salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation_\(Christianity\)) is only achieved through acceptance of Jesus as one's savior.[^cite_note-JeC3-257] Some members of Messianic Judaism argue that it is a sect of Judaism.[^cite_note-MJSelfID-258] Jewish organizations of every denomination reject this, stating that Messianic Judaism is a Christian sect, because it teaches creeds which are identical to those of [Pauline Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_Christianity), and because the conditions for the Messiah to have come accordingly within traditional Jewish thought have not yet been met.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-Denominations-260] Another religious movement is the [Black Hebrew Israelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hebrew_Israelite) group, which not to be confused with less syncretic [Black Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Judaism) (a constellation of movements which, depending on their adherence to normative Jewish tradition, receive varying degrees of recognition by the broader Jewish community).

Other examples of [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) include [Semitic neopaganism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_neopaganism), loosely organized sects which incorporate [pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism), [Goddess movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goddess_movement) or [Wiccan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wicca) beliefs with some Jewish religious practices;[^cite_note-RaphaelMelissa-261] [Jewish Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Buddhist), another loosely organized group that incorporates elements of [Buddhism](3267529.md) and other Asian spirituality in their faith.[^cite_note-262]

Some [Renewal Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Renewal) borrow freely and openly from Buddhism, [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism), [Native American religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_religions), and other faiths.[^cite_note-Magid2005-160][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESegal2008123–129-161]

The [Kabbalah Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah_Centre), which employs teachers from multiple religions, is a one of "[New Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Age) Judaism" movements[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeusnerAvery-Peck2003354–370"New_Age_Judaism"-263] that claims to popularize the [kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah), part of the [Jewish esoteric tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_mysticism).[^cite_note-264]

## Criticism

Main article: [Criticism of Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Judaism)

Criticism of Judaism may include those that require [revisionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_revisionism) to classical Orthodox Judaism, such as that of the modernized denomination of [Reconstructionist Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism) as established by American rabbi [Mordecai Kaplan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mordecai_Kaplan), who believed that classical Orthodox Judaism is outdated as a religious belief on its own, and should represent Judaism as a civilization.[^cite_note-delusion-265][^cite_note-266]
